, Theorem 3.9]
)
Moreover,
although the original notion
has been defined using open coverings, a standard argument
shows this Carathéodory structure can be defined using dynamic balls (see e.g.
[
59
]
), an object that suits our purposes better.

4.3.
Fibered topological entropies

Given
\varepsilon>0
and
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
consider the metric in
X_{\underline{\omega}}
given by

d_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{n}(x,y)=\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant n-1}d(g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x),g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(y))

for
x,y\in X_{\underline{\omega}}
and
n\geqslant 1
. The dynamic ball determined by
x\in X_{\omega}
,
n\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
is the set

B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n,\varepsilon)=\Big\{y\in X_{\underline{\omega}}:d_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{n}(x,y)<\varepsilon\;\;\mbox{for all}\;\;0\leqslant j<n.\Big\}

A subset
K\subset X_{{\underline{\omega}}}
is called
({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon)
-separated
if
d_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{n}(x,y)>\varepsilon

for any distinct
x,y\in K
.

We define the
fibered topological entropy for the semigroup
on the compact metric space
X

as

h^{path}(\mathbb{S})=\sup_{{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}}\,h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}}),F_{\underline{\omega}})

(4.7)

where

h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s(g_{\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon)

denotes the topological pressure of sequential dynamical system
\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}}=(g_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{j})_{j\geqslant 1}

introduced in
[
45
]
.
Related measure theoretical notions of fibered entropy appeared in early studies by Pinsker
[
60
]
, Abramov and Rokhlin
[
1
,
64
]
,
Ledrappier and Walters
[
52
]

on the entropy of skew-products and later associated to the context of random dynamical systems (see e.g.
[
44
]
).
Indeed, it is known that

h_{\mu}(F)=h_{\pi_{*}\mu}(\sigma)+h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)

(4.8)

for each
F
-invariant probability measure
\mu
, where
the relative entropy is defined by the supremum
h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)=\sup_{\xi}h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma,\xi)
over finite partitions
\xi
of

\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X
, where

h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma,\xi)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_{\mu}\Big(\bigvee_{j=0}^{n-1}F^{-j}\xi\mid\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{E})\Big)

and
\mathcal{E}
denotes the partition of
\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
into points.

Remark 4.2
.

The expression (
4.8
) is known as the Abramov-Rokhlin formula, although it is
sometimes referred as Pinsker formula in the special case that
\sigma
is invertible (cf.
[
60
,
64
]
).
It was proven for skew-products with non-invertible fiber maps by Bogenschütz and Crauel
[
11
]
.

Remark 4.3
.

Whenever
\mathbb{P}
is a
\sigma
-invariant probability and
\mu
is an
F
-invariant probability whose marginal
\pi_{*}\mu
in
\Sigma_{\kappa}

coincides with
\mathbb{P}
, the value
\int h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\,d\mathbb{P}
is the (quenched) topological entropy of
\mu

for the random dynamical system driven by
\mathbb{P}
(see
[
44
]
). Moreover,
h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)=h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F})

where
h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F})=\sup_{\xi}h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F},\xi)
,
the supremum is taken over all finite Borel partitions
\xi
of
X
,

h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F},\xi)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\int H_{\mu_{\underline{\omega}}}\Big(\bigvee_{k=0}^{n-1}(f^{k}_{\underline{\omega}})^{-1}\xi\Big)d\mathbb{P}({\underline{\omega}}),

and
\mu=(\mu_{\underline{\omega}})_{\underline{\omega}}
is the almost everywhere defined Rokhlin disintegration of
\mu
on its sample measures
(see e.g.
[
10
,
11
]
). The variational principle for entropy states that

\int h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\,d\mathbb{P}({\underline{\omega}})=\sup\Big\{h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F})\colon\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{inv}(F),\pi_{*}\mu=\mathbb{P}\Big\}

(cf.
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
).
We will consider the previous equality when
\mathbb{P}=\pi_{*}\mu
in order to compare the fibered entropies of

F
-invariant probabilities
\mu
.
In particular, when the
F
-invariant measure
\mu
changes then the entropies
h_{\mu}(F\mid\sigma)
are not related to any fixed
random dynamical system.
Nevertheless,

\int h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\,d\pi_{*}\mu\geqslant h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)\;\text{for every}\,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{inv}(F).

This, together with the ergodic decomposition theorem, ensures that

h^{path}(\mathbb{S})\geqslant\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{inv}(F)}\,h_{\mu}(F\mid\sigma)=\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(F)}\,h_{\mu}(F\mid\sigma)=:h_{\operatorname{top}}(F\mid\sigma),

(4.9)

an expression which relates the fibered entropy of the semigroup with that generated by
F
-invariant probabilities.

Let us now recall another notion of measure theoretical entropy for non-autonomous dynamical systems, which is adaptable to general probabilities,
but for which we will be consider with respect to the sample measures of
F
-invariant probabilities. Let us be more precise.
Assume that
\mu
is an
F
-invariant and ergodic probability, that
0<\delta<1
,
\varepsilon>0
and
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By compactness of
X_{\underline{\omega}}
, for each
Y\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
the largest cardinality
s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,Y)

of an
({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon)
-separated subset of
Y
is finite. Now, take
s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,\delta):=\min\{s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,Y):\mu(Y)\geqslant 1-\delta\}
and define

h_{\mu}^{\delta}(\underline{\omega}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,\delta),

(4.10)

which is clearly bounded above by
h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})
.
A related notion of entropy, using a Shannon-McMillan-Breiman type formula appeared in
[
9
]
.
The
Katok
\delta
-entropy
of
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{inv}(F)

is defined by

h_{\mu}^{\delta}({F})=\int h_{\mu}^{\delta}(\underline{\omega})\,d(\pi_{*}\mu)({\underline{\omega}}).

In the special case that
\mu
is ergodic one has that

h_{\mu}^{\delta}({F})=h_{\mu}^{\delta}(\underline{\omega})=h_{\mu}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})=h_{\mu}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)\quad\text{for $(\pi_{*}\mu)$-a.e. ${\underline{\omega}}$}

(4.11)

coincides with the quenched topological entropy of the random dynamical system
driven by
\mathbb{P}=\pi_{*}\mu
(cf.
[
47
, Theorem A]
). In particular we conclude that

H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)=H^{Katok}(\psi),

(4.12)

where the left hand-side was defined in (
2.7
) and the second term is defined by

\displaystyle H^{Katok}(\psi):=\sup\Big\{c\geqslant 0\colon

\displaystyle\forall\varepsilon>0\,\text{there exist}\,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(F)\,\text{so that}\,

\displaystyle h_{\mu_{i}}^{\delta}(F)\geqslant c-\varepsilon\;\text{and}\,\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}\Big\}.

(4.13)

In the case of non-compact subsets
Y\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
,
Stadlbauer, Suzuki and the second named author
[
65
, Appendix A]
introduced a notion of relative entropy using Carathéodory structures in the realm of random dynamical systems.
Let us recall this notion.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Omega
and consider the sets

{\mathcal{I}}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}=X_{\underline{\omega}}\times\{n\}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{\underline{\omega}}=X_{\underline{\omega}}\times\mathbb{N}
. For
every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\geqslant 1
, define

m_{\alpha}({\underline{\omega}},f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon,N)=\inf_{\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}}\Big\{\sum_{{(x,n)}\in\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}}e^{-\alpha n(x)+S_{n(x)}\phi_{\underline{\omega}}({\mathrm{B}_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n(x),\varepsilon)})}\Big\},

(4.14)

where the infimum is taken over all finite or countable families

\mathcal{G}
of the form
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}\subset\cup_{n\geqslant N}{\mathcal{I}}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}

such that the collection
of sets
\{B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n,\varepsilon):(x,n)\in\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}\}
cover
\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}}

and such that (
4.14
) is measurable in
\omega
.
As before,

S_{n}\phi_{\underline{\omega}}({\mathrm{B}_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n,\varepsilon)})=\sup\{\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\phi_{\theta^{j}({\underline{\omega}})}(g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(y)):y\in B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n,\varepsilon)\}.

Using that the previous sequence is increasing on
N
, the limit

m_{\alpha}({\underline{\omega}},f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon)=\lim_{N\to+\infty}m_{\alpha}({\underline{\omega}},f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon,N)

(4.15)

does exist. If
\alpha
is such that (
4.15
) is finite and
\beta>\alpha
then

m_{\beta}(f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon,N)\leqslant e^{-(\beta-\alpha)N}m_{\alpha}(f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon,N)
tends to zero as
N\to\infty
, hence

m_{\beta}(f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon)=0
. For that reason we define

\pi_{\phi}({\underline{\omega}},f,\Lambda,\varepsilon)=\inf{\{\alpha:m_{\alpha}({\underline{\omega}},f,\phi,\Lambda,\varepsilon)=0\}},

which is decreasing on
\varepsilon
.
The fibered
relative pressure
of the set
\Lambda
with respect to

(f,\phi)
is defined by

\pi_{\phi}({\underline{\omega}},f,\Lambda)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\pi_{\phi}({\underline{\omega}},f,\Lambda,\varepsilon).

(4.16)

The previous limit exists as
\varepsilon\mapsto\pi_{\phi}({\underline{\omega}},f,\Lambda,\varepsilon)
is non-increasing.
In the special case that
\phi\equiv 0
we write simply

h_{\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega}):=\pi_{0}({\underline{\omega}},f,\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}}).

It is not hard to check that
h_{\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega})\leqslant h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})
for every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

In general, upper bounds are much easier to obtain as these rely on choices of certain coverings of
\Lambda
using dynamic balls at a certain scale.
The next result provides an entropy distribution principle for fibered sets, which is a simple modification of
[
71
, Theorem 3.6]

and
[
76
, Proposition 2.6]
for sequential dynamical systems and serves as a criterion to provide lower bounds on these fibered entropies.

Proposition 4.4

(Fibered entropy distribution principle)
.

Let
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}}\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
be an arbitrary Borel set.
Suppose there exist
\varepsilon>0
,
\alpha\geqslant 0
,
K_{\underline{\omega}}(\varepsilon)>0
and a sequence of probability measures
(\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k})_{k\geqslant 1}
on
X_{\underline{\omega}}
such that

\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{\underline{\omega},k}(B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n,\varepsilon))\leqslant K_{\underline{\omega}}(\varepsilon)\,e^{-\alpha n}

(4.17)

for sufficiently large
n
and every ball
B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n,\varepsilon)
which has non-empty intersection with
\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}
.
If there exists a weak
∗
accumulation point
\nu_{\underline{\omega}}
of the probability measures
(\mu_{\underline{\omega},k})_{k\geqslant 1}
so that

\nu_{\underline{\omega}}(\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}})>0
then

h_{\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega})\geqslant\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\alpha\geqslant 0
,
K_{\underline{\omega}}(\varepsilon)>0
be as above, and assume that
(\mu_{\underline{\omega},k_{j}})_{j\geqslant 1}
is weak
∗
convergent
to
\nu_{{\underline{\omega}}}
. Assume also that
\nu_{\underline{\omega}}(\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}})>0
. Fix
N_{\underline{\omega}}\geqslant 1
large so that (
4.17
) holds for all
n\geqslant N_{\underline{\omega}}
.
Consider an arbitrary countable family
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}\subset\cup_{n\geqslant N_{\underline{\omega}}}{\mathcal{I}}_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}
such that the collection

\{B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,n(x),\varepsilon):(x,n(x))\in\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}\}
covers
\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}}
. Extracting a subcollection of sets, if necessary, one may assume without loss of generality that

B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n(x),\varepsilon)\cap\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}\neq\emptyset
for every
(x,n(x))\in\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}
.
Therefore, by the Portmanteau theorem on the characterization of weak
∗
convergence one concludes that

\displaystyle\sum_{{(x,n)}\in\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}}e^{-\alpha n(x)}

\displaystyle\geqslant K_{{\underline{\omega}}}(\varepsilon)^{-1}\sum_{i}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}(B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n(x),\varepsilon))

\displaystyle\geqslant K_{\omega}(\varepsilon)^{-1}\sum_{i}\nu_{{\underline{\omega}}}(B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n(x),\varepsilon))

\displaystyle\geqslant K_{{\underline{\omega}}}(\varepsilon)^{-1}\nu(\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}})>0

As
\mathcal{G}_{\underline{\omega}}
was chosen arbitrary this implies that
m_{\alpha}({\underline{\omega}},f,0,\Lambda_{\underline{\omega}},\varepsilon,N)\geqslant K_{{\underline{\omega}}}(\varepsilon)^{-1}\nu(\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}})>0

for every
N\geqslant N_{\underline{\omega}}
and, consequently,

h_{\Lambda_{{\underline{\omega}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega})\geqslant\alpha
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

We observe that
Proposition
4.4
is of independent interest in the special case of random dynamical systems, modeled by the skew-product
F
. Indeed,
it allows to estimate the entropy of equivariant sets contained in fibers
X_{\underline{\omega}}
associated to a
\mathbb{P}
-generic point
{\underline{\omega}}

provided the existence of
F
-invariant probabilities
(\mu_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
whose disintegrations
(\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, defined almost everywhere
by Rokhlin theorem as a measurable family
{\underline{\omega}}\mapsto\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}
of probabilities so that
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
and

\mu=\int\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}\,d\mathbb{P}({\underline{\omega}})
, satisfy (
4.17
) at a certain scale
\varepsilon
and every large
n
.

4.4.
Some examples

In the remainder of this section we compare the quantities
h_{*}(\varphi_{\psi})
,
H^{\text{KS}}(\psi)

H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)
and
H^{\sigma}(\psi)
defined by
(
2.5
)-(
2.8
), using a couple of examples. For the sake of simplicity we shall consider semigroups generated by certain continuous maps on the interval
[0,1]
.

Example 4.6
.

Let
G
be the semigroup action generated by two continuous and Markov interval maps
f_{1},f_{2}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
as described in the following figure.
Let
\psi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous observable.

Figure 4.1.
Generators
G_{1}=\{f_{1},f_{2}\}

Assume further that
\psi
is not cohomologous to a constant neither with respect to
f_{1}
or
f_{2}

(in particular
\varphi_{\psi}
is not cohomologous to a constant with respect to the step skew-product
F
on
\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,1]
determined by
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Assume, additionally, that
\psi\mid_{[a,1]}\equiv 1
.

Firstly we compute the entropy of the skew-product
F
.
The map
F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]}
is a distance expanding map and is semiconjugate to a full shift with
4
symbols, hence

h_{\operatorname{top}}(F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]})=\log 4
.
Proceeding with a similar description of the three transitive components and Markov property of the skew-product it is easy to check that

h_{\operatorname{top}}(F)=\log 4
.
As
F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]}
satisfies the specification property then the space of ergodic probability measures is entropy dense, hence
the assumptions on
\psi
guarantee that there exist
F
-invariant and ergodic probability measures

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
with entropy arbitrarily close to
\log 4
so that
\int\varphi_{\psi}d\mu_{1}\neq\int\varphi_{\psi}d\mu_{2}
, hence

h_{\operatorname{top}}(F)=\log 4=h_{*}(\varphi_{\psi}).

Secondly, let us observe now the non-autonomous dynamical systems generated by concatenations of maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. The restriction of each of
these maps to any of its invariant intervals
\{[0,a],[a,b],[b,1]\}
defines a continuous, piecewise affine distance expanding and Markov map with degree at most three. Hence,

h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\leqslant\log 3\quad\text{for every }{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{2}^{+}.

Actually,

H^{KS}(\psi)=\log 3,

where the maximum is attained for any
{\underline{\omega}}
in the pre-orbit of the fixed point
(2,2,2,\dots)\in\Sigma_{2}^{+}
.

Finally, let us now draw our attention to the Pinsker entropy and the contribution on the shift of measures with large fibered entropy. Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be two

F
-invariant and ergodic probabilities so that
\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}
.
As
\psi
is constant on the interval
[a,b]
, both probabilities cannot be simultaneously supported on the invariant domain
\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[a,b]
and, using (
4.11
), we deduce that

h_{\mu_{i}}(F\mid\sigma)\leqslant\log 2
for either
i=1
or
i=2
. We claim that
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)=\log 2
. Indeed, this follows as a standard consequence of the specification property for
F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]}
(which implies that ergodic measures are entropy dense) combined with the fact that the unique maximal entropy
\mu^{1}_{max}
measure for
F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]}
projects
over the Bernoulli measure
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{\mathbb{N}}
on
\Sigma_{2}^{+}
and, consequently,

h_{\mu^{1}_{max}}(F\mid\sigma)=h_{\mu^{1}_{max}}(F)-h_{\pi_{*}\mu^{1}_{max}}(\sigma)=\log 4-\log 2=\log 2.

Altogether,

H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)=\log 2<\log 3=H^{KS}(\psi).

Furthermore, assume that the unique maximal entropy
\mu^{2}_{max}
measure for
F\mid_{\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[a,b]}
is such that

\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu^{1}_{max}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu^{2}_{max}
(otherwise use entropy denseness of ergodic measures to produce
an ergodic measure arbitrarily close to
\mu^{2}_{max}
with fibered entropy arbitrary close to
\log 3
). As this measure projects over
the Bernoulli measure
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{\mathbb{N}}
as well we conclude that

H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\log 2=h_{\operatorname{top}}(\sigma).

Example 4.7
.

Let
G
be the semigroup generated by the continuous and Markov interval maps
f_{2},f_{3}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
described in the previous figure, and let

\hat{F}:\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,1]\to\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,1]
denote the step skew-product determined by them.
Let
\psi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous observable so that
\psi\mid_{[a,b]}\equiv 1
and that
is not cohomologous to a constant neither with respect to
f_{2}
or
f_{3}
(in particular
\varphi_{\psi}
is not cohomologous to a constant with respect to
\hat{F}
.
It is easy to check that

h_{\operatorname{top}}(\hat{F})=\log 6
. Moreover, as
\psi
is constant over the interval
[a,b]
, given two
\hat{F}
-invariant and ergodic probabilities
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
so that

\int\varphi_{\psi}d\mu_{1}\neq\int\varphi_{\psi}d\mu_{2}
at least one of them is supported either on
\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[0,a]
or
\Sigma_{2}^{+}\times[b,1]
.
Using the specification property for the restriction of
\hat{F}
to any of these invariant domains, one gets that

h_{\operatorname{top}}(\hat{F})=\log 6>\log 4\geqslant h_{*}(\varphi_{\psi}).

and
H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\log 2=h_{\operatorname{top}}(\sigma).

Finally, it is worthwhile to point out that the entropy estimates in Examples
4.6
and
4.7
involve counting elements in a
Markov partition and the fact that all compositions are also Markov. Hence, it is likely that similar examples can be produced
involving higher dimensional piecewise expanding maps or even Axiom A diffeomorphisms on a compact Riemannian manifold.

5.
The frequent hitting times property

In this section we not only provide sufficient conditions to ensure that
single dynamics, semigroup actions and skew-products satisfy the notion of frequent hitting times,
as we discuss the relation between it, some weak versions of specification and hyperbolicity.

5.1.
Sufficient condition for the frequent hitting times property

Our aim here is to provide a checkable condition which implies on the frequent hitting times property, and to provide
several examples that illustrate its applicability.
Assume that the semigroup action generated by a collection
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
of continuous maps on a compact metric space
X
satisfying the following
strong transitivity
property: for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
K(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
such that:

\forall x\in X\,\exists\,{\underline{\omega}}={\underline{\omega}}_{x}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\qquad\bigcup_{j=0}^{K(\varepsilon)}\,f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(B(x,\varepsilon))=X.

(C)

In the special case that the continuous maps
f_{i}
are actually homeomorphisms
it will be often more convenient to express the previous condition (C) using their inverses:
for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
K(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
, and for each
x\in X
there exists
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}

such that

\forall x\in X\,\exists\,{\underline{\omega}}={\underline{\omega}}_{x}\,\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\qquad\bigcup_{j=0}^{K(\varepsilon)}\,f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{0}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{-1}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{K(\varepsilon)}}^{-1}(B(x,\varepsilon))=X.

(C’)

Conditions (C) and (C’) are clearly equivalent for semigroup actions generated by isometries. Actually, we have the following:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space and consider the semigroup action
\mathbb{S}
generated by a collection of
homeomorphisms
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
satisfying property (C).
If the smallest sub-semigroup action satisfying property (C) is
generated by a collection
\widetilde{G_{1}}\subset G_{1}
of isometries
then the semigroup action
\mathbb{S}

has frequent hitting times.

Proof.

Assume there exists a collection
\widetilde{G_{1}}\subset G_{1}
of isometries generating a sub-semigroup action
satisfying (C), and for any
\varepsilon>0
let
K(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
be given according to this property.
We claim that the semigroup action generated by
G_{1}
has frequent hitting times.
Fix arbitrary balls
(B_{1},B_{2})
with
B_{1}=B(x_{1},\varepsilon)
and
B_{2}
of radius
0<r_{2}<\varepsilon/2
.
If
\hat{B}_{1}\subset B_{1}
is the ball of radius
\varepsilon/3
centered at
x_{1}

then there exists
0\leqslant p\leqslant K(\varepsilon/3)
, a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p}
and elements
f_{\omega_{i}}\in\widetilde{G_{1}}
for every

0\leqslant i\leqslant p
so that
f_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}(\hat{B}_{1})\cap B_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
By the hypothesis, the map

f_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}
is an isometry. Therefore, if

r_{2}<\varepsilon/3
then
f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(B_{1})\supset B_{2}
. Otherwise,
\varepsilon/3\leqslant r_{2}<\varepsilon/2
and
f_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}(B_{1})\cap B_{2}
contains a ball of radius

\frac{\varepsilon}{3}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\frac{2}{3}>r_{2}\frac{2}{3}>\frac{r_{2}}{2}
.
Altogether, this shows that
G_{1}
satisfies the frequent hitting time property with transition time

K(\varepsilon/3)
, and proves the lemma.
∎

In other words, the previous lemma ensures that the frequent hitting times property
hold for semigroup actions which contain some minimal action by isometries.
Recall that a homeomorphism
f:X\to X
is
minimal
if all points have a dense orbit, and
a semigroup action generated by a collection of homeomorphisms
G_{1}
is
minimal
if for every
x\in X
there
exists
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
so that
X\,=\,\overline{\{f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(x)\colon n\geqslant 0\}}

(cf.
[
39
]
).
The previous lemma can be reformulated to provide some classes of examples, defined in terms of minimality, which satisfy the frequent hitting times property.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
X
be a compact metric space. The semigroup action generated by the collection
G_{1}
of continuous maps
has frequent hitting times in the following cases:

(1)

G_{1}=\{f\}
, where
f:X\to X
is a minimal isometry;

(2)

G_{1}=\{f\}
, where
f:\mathbf{S}^{1}\to\mathbf{S}^{1}
is an irrational rotation;

(3)

G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
and there exists a sub-collection

\widetilde{G_{1}}\subset G_{1}
formed by isometries which generate a minimal semigroup action. .

Proof.

Items (1) and (2) are immediate consequences of item (3), hence we are left to prove the latter.
Moreover, using Lemma
5.1
it is enough to show that
the collection
\widetilde{G_{1}}
generates a sub-semigroup satisfying property (C).
The argument is identical to the one used in the proof of
[
68
, Lemma 5.1]
adapted to the semigroup actions context, which we include
for completeness. Indeed, by minimality of the sub-semigroup action,
for any
x\in X
there exists
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
so that
f_{\omega_{i}}\in\widetilde{G_{1}}
for every
i\geqslant 0

and

X\,=\,\overline{\{f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n}(x)\colon n\geqslant 0\}}.

Thus, for every
\varepsilon>0
we obtain that

\bigcup_{n\geqslant 0}(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n})^{-1}(B(x,\varepsilon))\,=\,X.

Moreover, by compactness of
X
,
there exists
K_{x}(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
so that

\bigcup_{n=0}^{K_{x}(\varepsilon)}(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n})^{-1}(B(x,\varepsilon))\,=\,X.

Now, using the uniform continuity of the finite collection of maps
f_{\omega_{i}}
, there exists
\delta_{x}>0
so that

\bigcup_{n=0}^{K_{x}(\varepsilon)}(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{n})^{-1}(B(y,\varepsilon))\,=\,X\quad\text{for every $y\in B(x,\delta_{x})$}.

Extracting a finite sub cover
\{B(x_{i},\delta_{i}):1\leqslant i\leqslant s\}\subset\{B(x,\delta_{x}):x\in X\}
, we conclude that
(C’) holds with

K(\varepsilon)=\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant s}K_{x_{i}}(\varepsilon)
.
Furthermore,
as each elements
f_{\omega_{i}}
is an isometry, we conclude that property (C) holds for the sub-semigroup action generated by
\widetilde{G_{1}}
,
which completes the proof of the corollary.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

In the special case that
f:\mathbf{S}^{1}\to\mathbf{S}^{1}
is an irrational rotation
it is easy to check from the proof of Lemma
5.1
and simple estimates on the minimal covering time
(cf.
[
13
]
) that the frequent hitting times property holds with

K(\varepsilon)=[\frac{3}{\varepsilon}]+1
.
In particular, the transition time may be exponentially large on balls with exponentially small radius.

Remark 5.4
.

In the case of a single homeomorphism, one can replace the assumption that the dynamics
f
is an isometry
by equicontinuity of the family
\{f^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, as in this context
d_{f}(x,y):=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}d(f^{n}(x),f^{n}(y))
defines a metric
on
X
with respect to which
f
becomes an isometry.

Remark 5.5
.

The frequent hitting times property can be rephrased as follows: for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
K(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1

so that for any balls
(B_{1},B_{2})
such that
|B_{1}|=\varepsilon
there exists
0\leqslant p\leqslant K(\varepsilon)
,
{\underline{\omega}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p}
and a ball
B_{2}^{\prime}\subset B_{2}
of radius

r\geqslant\min\{|B_{1}|/4,|B_{2}|/2\}
so that
g_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}(B_{1})\supset B_{2}^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
|B_{2}|>\varepsilon/2
and one takes
\hat{B}_{2}\subset B_{2}
a ball of radius
|B_{1}|/2

then there exists
B_{2}^{\prime}\subset\hat{B}_{2}
of radius

|\hat{B}_{2}|/2=|B_{1}|/4
so that
g_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}(B_{1})\supset B_{2}^{\prime}
.

Let us provide a simple example which fits in the context of item (3) in the corollary.

Example 5.6
.

Consider the torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, let
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a base of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that the components of each vector
v_{i}
are rationally independent. For each
i=1,2,3
consider the translation

f_{i}(x)=x+v_{i}(mod\,\mathbb{Z}^{3})
,
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, which is clearly an isometry.
Even though each individual translation may fail to be transitive, the choice of the vectors
v_{i}
guarantees that the semigroup action generated by
\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
is minimal. According to Corollary
5.2
,
the semigroup action generated by
G_{1}=\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
has frequent hitting times.

5.2.
Projective linear maps

In opposition to the case of the torus, where a single irrational translation have dense orbits, minimal semigroup action in arbitrary
manifolds often require some minimum number of generators (see
[
39
]
and references therein for a discussion on this topic).
This is precisely the case for continuous maps on spheres, which appear naturally in applications to linear cocycles as these are
double covering of real projective Euclidean spaces.

Example 5.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the unit sphere an
d, given
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
consider the semigroup action on
{\mathbf{S}}^{2}
generated by the collection
G_{1}=\{f_{0},f_{1}\}

of elements in
SO(3)
defined as

f_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\cos\alpha&-\sin\alpha&0\\
\sin\alpha&\cos\alpha&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad f_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&\cos\beta&-\sin\beta\\
0&\sin\beta&\cos\beta\end{array}\right)

The maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are irrational rotations around the
z
-axis and the
x
-axis, respectively.
It is clear from the use of polar coordinates
and the fact that the rotations are irrational that the semigroup action generated by the collection
G_{1}=\{f_{1},f_{2}\}
of isometries is minimal.
Corollary
5.2
implies that such semigroup action has frequent hitting times.

Example 5.8
.

Let
Sp(2\ell,\mathbb{R})
denote the Lie group of
2\ell\times 2\ell
-symplectic matrices.
Assume that
A\in sp(4,\mathbb{R})
is a
4\times 4
symplectic matrix corresponding to a generic center, i.e.

A
has two pairs of non-real, complex conjugate eigenvalues with norm one.
By continuity of the isolated eigenvalues,
there exists an open neighborhood
\mathscr{U}\subset sp(4,\mathbb{R})
of the matrix
A

formed by symplectic matrices having distinct unitary complex eigenvalues and, up to conjugacy by symplectic matrices, each
B\in\mathscr{U}

is determined by their eigenvalues
in the canonical symplectic basis
where the
2
-form can be written as
e_{1}\wedge\hat{e}_{1}+e_{2}\wedge\hat{e}_{2}
,
one can write

B=\begin{pmatrix}a&0&-b&0\\
0&c&0&-d\\
b&0&a&0\\
0&d&0&c\end{pmatrix}

where
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
,
c^{2}+d^{2}=1
and
b,d\neq 0
. Up to conjugacy by a symplectic change of basis matrix, the previous matrices are in one-to-one correspondance with the previous parameterization of its eigenvalues
a\pm{\bf i}\,b
and
c\pm{\bf i}\,d
. The projectivization of
B
is
an isometry. Moreover, there exists a Baire generic and full Haar measure subset
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathscr{U}

so that the eigenvalues of each
B\in\mathcal{R}
do not satisfy any of the equations

(a+bi)^{m}\,(c+di)^{n}=1,

with
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
and, consequently,
the projectivization of
B
is a minimal isometry on
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
The previous reasoning can also be action on the central direction of to partially hyperbolic
sl(2\ell,\mathbb{R})
matrices (
\ell>2
)
having a generic center in its 4-dimensional center subbundle.

5.3.
Relation with other gluing orbit properties and hyperbolicity

Here we provide some examples to illustrate that the frequent hitting times property is significantly different from
the gluing orbit and specification properties. Indeed, The specification property is frequently associated to hyperbolic dynamics and
the gluing orbit property can arise in both hyperbolic, partially hyperbolic and non-uniformly hyperbolic dynamics
(we refer the reader
[
15
,
14
]
for definitions and precise statements). However, as observed in Subsection
5.1
, the frequent hitting times property
is guaranteed for minimal semigroup actions by isometries.
The next examples illustrate that it may occur even if all others notions fail.

We say that the semigroup action generated by
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},...,f_{\kappa}\}
satisfies the
gluing orbit property
if for any

\varepsilon>0
there exists
K(\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
so that for any points
(x_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{1}),\dots(x_{r},{\underline{\omega}}_{r})
in
X\times\Sigma_{\kappa}

there exist
x\in X
,
0\leqslant p_{i}\leqslant K(\varepsilon)
and
\theta_{i}\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}^{p_{i}}
(
i=1\dots r-1
) so that

d(f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x),f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1}))<\varepsilon
for every
j=1,...,n_{1}
and

d(f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}}\dots f_{\theta_{2}}^{p_{2}}{f}^{n_{2}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(x)f_{\theta_{1}}^{p_{1}}{f}^{n_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x),\,f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}}(x_{j}))<\varepsilon

for every
j=2,...,n_{\ell}
and
\ell=1,...,k
.
The first example
shows that the frequent hitting times property
may fail among hyperbolic dynamics.

Example 5.9
.

Consider the linear Anosov automorphism
f_{A}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
induced by

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&1\\
1&1\end{array}\right)

and let
q:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
be the quotient map. In particular,
f_{A}
satisfies the gluing orbit property
[
16
]
.
Note that
p=(0,0)
is a hyperbolic fixed point for
f
, and that the
A
-invariant hyperbolic splitting
\mathbb{R}^{2}=E^{u}\oplus E^{s}

is such that the invariant manifolds at
p
are simply
W^{*}(p)=q(E^{*})
where
*\in\{s,u\}
. Let
\lambda>1
be the leading eigenvalue for

A
(and, since
A
is volume preserving,
\lambda^{-1}
is the contracting eigenvalue for
A
).
Given
\varepsilon>0
, the set
f_{A}^{n}(B(p,\varepsilon))
is the projection by
q
of an ellipsis in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose larger and smaller axis have length

\lambda^{n}\varepsilon
and
\lambda^{-n}\varepsilon
, respectively, wrapped in the torus.
In consequence, the inner diameter of the set
f_{A}^{n}(B(p,\varepsilon))
decreases exponentially fast with
n
,
hence the frequent hitting times property does not hold.

Example 5.10
.

Consider the semigroup action
\mathbb{S}
generated by the homeomorphisms
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2}\}
on the circle
\mathbf{S}^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
where
f_{0}
is a north-pole south-pole diffeomorphism and
f_{1}
a irrational rotation (see Figure 5.1 below).
By
[
13
]
and Corollary
5.2
, the map
f_{1}
satisfies both the gluing orbit and the frequent hitting times properties. In particular the semigroup action
\mathbb{S}
has frequent hitting times.

Figure 5.1.
Iterated function system with two generators
f_{0},f_{1}
on the circle
\mathbf{S}^{1}
: a north-pole south-pole homeomorphism and an irrational rotation
f_{1}

We proceed to show that such semigroup action does not satisfy a gluing orbit property nor any non-uniform specification property.
First, since
f_{1}
is an irrational rotation then the orbit of
\frac{1}{2}
cannot
contain
0
(otherwise a composition of these rotations would be a rotation of rational angle
1/2
).
For any
\delta>0
let
k(\delta)\geqslant 1
be the largest integer so that
d(f_{1}^{j}(\frac{1}{2}),1)>2\delta
for every

0\leqslant j\leqslant k(\delta)
. Clearly
k(\delta)\to+\infty
as
\delta\to 0
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
x_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
and
x_{2}=0
. By the uniformly contracting (resp. expanding) behavior in a neighborhood of
x_{1}
(resp.
x_{2}
),
there exists
\lambda\in(0,1)
so that

f_{0}^{n}(B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon))\subset B(x_{1},\lambda^{n}\varepsilon)
and
B_{f_{0}}(x_{2},n,\varepsilon)\subset B(x_{2},\lambda^{n}\varepsilon)
for every small
\varepsilon>0
and every
n\geqslant 1
. Observe that the contracting behavior in a neighborhood of
x_{1}
guarantees that
f_{0}^{(j+1)+n}(B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon))\subset f_{0}^{j+n}(B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon))
and
f_{0}^{j+n}(B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon))\cap B_{f_{0}}(x_{2},n,\varepsilon)=\emptyset
, for every
j\geqslant 0
.
Hence, any possible orbit between the sets
B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon)
and
B_{f_{0}}(x_{2},n,\varepsilon)
need to arise from a concatenation of the map
f_{1}

for a certain number of times. By construction,

f_{1}^{j}f_{0}^{n}(B_{f_{0}}(x_{1},n,\varepsilon))\cap B_{f_{0}}(x_{2},n,\varepsilon)=\emptyset\quad\text{for every $0\leqslant j\leqslant k(\lambda^{n}\varepsilon)$}

and, consequently, not only any such transition time depends on the size
n
of the orbit of
x_{1}
as it grows exponentially fast with
n
. Indeed,
by
[
13
]

the minimal integer
\lfloor\frac{1}{\delta}\rfloor-1\leqslant K_{0}(\delta)\leqslant\lfloor\frac{1}{\delta}\rfloor+1

so that
\bigcup_{j=0}^{K_{0}(\delta)}f_{1}^{j}(B(z,\delta))=\mathbf{S}^{1}
for every
z\in\mathbf{S}^{1}

is bounded above by
2K_{1}(\delta)
where
K_{1}(\delta)\geqslant 1
is the smallest integer so that
f_{1}^{j}(B(z,\delta))\cap B(z,\delta)=\emptyset

for every
z\in\mathbf{S}^{1}
(as
f_{1}
is a minimal isometry the integers
K_{0}(\delta),K_{1}(\delta)
are independent of the point
z
).
As
0
and
\frac{1}{2}
are in diametrically opposite positions one has that
k(\delta)\geqslant\lfloor\frac{1}{2}K_{1}(\frac{\delta}{2})\rfloor\geqslant\lfloor\frac{1}{\delta}\rfloor-1
for every small
\delta>0
, which ensures that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log k(\lambda^{n}\varepsilon)\geqslant-\log\lambda>0
. In particular the semigroup does not satisfy a non-uniform specification property,
ie, the transition times cannot be taken assumed to grow in a sub-linear way in comparison to the size of shadowed orbits.

Remark 5.11
.

The previous example has similar implications for the corresponding skew-product maps. Indeed, assume that

F:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbf{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbf{S}^{1}
is a

C^{1}
partially hyperbolic diffeomorphism of the form
F(x,y)=(A(x),g(x,y))
where
A:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
is an Anosov diffeomorphism and that there exists
p_{j}\in Per(A)
of period
\pi(p_{j})\geqslant 1

such that
F^{\pi(p_{j})}(p_{j},\cdot):\mathbf{S}^{1}\to\mathbf{S}^{1}
coincides with the map
f_{j}
(
j=0,1
).
Since
F
has periodic points of different index then it does not satisfy the gluing orbit property
[
14
]
.
While non-uniform specification holds for typical points of hyperbolic measures
[
56
]
, the
previous example suggests that a global non-uniform specification property fails dramatically.

In the remainder of this subsection we discuss the strength of the frequent hitting times in the process of shadowing finite pieces of orbits, with the specification, gluing orbit property and non-uniform specification. In fact, instead of being bounded, or even having sublinear growth with respect to the size of the finite pieces of orbits, transition times may be much larger than the size of the finite pieces of orbits: given a finite collection
(x_{i},n_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell}
, the frequent hitting times property guarantees that that there exist
p_{i}\geqslant 1
, depending on
\varepsilon
, (
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell-1
) and
x\in X
so that

0<n_{1}\leqslant n_{1}+p_{1}\leqslant n_{2}+(n_{1}+p_{1})\leqslant\dots\leqslant n_{\ell}+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(n_{i}+p_{i})

(5.1)

where

(1)

during the shadowing intervals
[\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(n_{i}+p_{i}),n_{j}+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(n_{i}+p_{i})]
the orbit of
x
remains

\varepsilon
-close to the orbit of the point
x_{j}

(2)

the transition interval
[n_{j}+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(n_{i}+p_{i}),n_{j}+p_{j}+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(n_{i}+p_{i})]
has size
p_{j}

(3)

p_{j}
may have exponential growth with respect to
n_{j}
.

Figure 5.2.
Time lags and oscillating behavior between shadowing finite pieces of orbits under:
(1) specification, (2) gluing orbit property, (3) non-uniform specification, and (4) frequent hitting time property.

Yet, while
p_{i}
may be much larger than
n_{i}
the crucial fact is that it has memory loss,
meaning it depends exlusively on it and not on the size of future pieces of orbits.
This makes possible to choose an adequate size

n_{j+1}
of an orbit and bookkeeping the previous transition times.
The effect obtained in the construction of points with oscillating time averages is illustrated in Figures 5.2 and 5.3.
We refer the reader to Section
6
and expressions (
6.4
) for more details.

Figure 5.3.
Illustration of the oscillating behavior of time averages along the shadowing of finite pieces of orbits for maps satisfying:
(1) specification, (2) gluing orbit property, (3) non-uniform specification, and (4) frequent hitting time property.

6.
The set of irregular points for semigroup actions is Baire generic

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
C
. In order to ease the presentation, we first explain how the assumptions in the
theorem allow one to construct points in
X
and an infinite path on the semigroup along which such point has divergent Birkhoff averages for the continuous
observable
\psi
(Subsection
6.1
). Exploring these ideas, we then prove that the set of non-typical points for the skew-product
F
and the
observable
\varphi_{\psi}
forms a Baire generic subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
(Subsection
6.2
).
The proof of the theorem is completed in Subsection
6.3
.

6.1.
The set of irregular points is non-empty

Assume that
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
is a collection of bi-Lipschitz homeomorphisms on a compact metric
space
X
and that
\psi:X\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous observable which is not a coboundary with respect to some
of the elements in
G_{1}
.
Assume, without loss of generality, that

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(f_{\kappa})}\int\psi\,d\mu<\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(f_{\kappa})}\int\psi\,d\mu.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be
f_{\kappa}
-invariant and ergodic probability measures such that
\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}<\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}

and take
x_{i}\in B(\mu_{i})
be points in the ergodic basin of attraction with respect to
f_{\kappa}
, for
i=1,2
. In particular, there are integers
1\leqslant n_{1}<n_{2}
so that

\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{\kappa}^{j}(x_{i}))-\int\psi\,d\mu_{i}\Big|<\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{8}\quad\text{for every}\,n\geqslant n_{i}

(6.1)

for
i=1,2
.
By uniform continuity of
\psi
, there one may choose
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|<\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{8}\quad\text{for every $x,y\in X$ so that $d(x,y)<\varepsilon_{0}$.}

(6.2)

To ease the notation, for each
x\in X
,
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geqslant 1
denote by
B_{f_{\kappa}}(x,n,\varepsilon)
the dynamic ball taken with respect to
f_{\kappa}
, that is,

B_{f_{\kappa}}(x,n,\varepsilon):=\big\{y\in X\colon d(f_{\kappa}^{j}(y),f_{\kappa}^{j}(x))\leqslant\varepsilon\;\text{for every}\;0\leqslant j\leqslant n-1\big\}.

Since the semigroup action satisfies the frequent hitting time property, by Remark
5.5
,
for every
\xi>0
there exists
K(\xi)\geqslant 1
so that for any balls
B_{1},B_{2}
in
X
such that
|B_{1}|=\xi
there exists

0\leqslant p\leqslant K(\xi)
,
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
and a ball
B_{2}^{\prime}\subset B_{2}
of radius

r\geqslant\min\{|B_{1}|/4,|B_{2}|/2\}
so that
g_{\underline{\omega}}^{p}(B_{1})\supset B_{2}^{\prime}
.

The idea is to use the frequent hitting time property to create points with oscillating time averages by making them
shadow pieces of orbits associated with points with distinct time averages.
The following auxiliary lemma provides a control on the size of dynamic balls and images of dynamic balls that
will be instrumental in the argument.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
f
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and
L>1
is a Lipschitz constant for both
f
and
f^{-1}
then

B_{f}(x,n,\varepsilon)\supset B(x,L^{-n}\varepsilon)\quad\text{and}\quad f^{n}(B_{f}(x,n,\varepsilon))\supset B(f^{n}(x),L^{-2n}\varepsilon)

(6.3)

for any
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

If
x,y\in X
and
d(x,y)<L^{-n}\varepsilon
then

d(f^{j}(x),f^{j}(y))\leqslant L^{j}d(x,y)\leqslant L^{j-n}\varepsilon<\varepsilon

for every
0\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
. This proves that
B_{f}(x,n,\varepsilon)\supset B(x,L^{-n}\varepsilon)
. Analogously, if

y\in B(f^{n}(x),L^{-2n}\varepsilon)
then
d(f^{-j}(y),f^{n-j}(x))\leqslant L^{j}d(y,f^{n}(x))\leqslant L^{j-2n}\varepsilon<L^{-n}\varepsilon
for every

0\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
.
This ensures that

B(f^{n}(x),L^{-2n}\varepsilon)\subset f^{n}(B(x,L^{-n}\varepsilon))\subset f^{n}(B_{f}(x,n,\varepsilon)),

which finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The previous lemma holds more generally of
X
is a compact and connected metric space and
f
is
a Lipschitz continuous local homeomorphism whose inverse branches are Lipschitz continuous. Indeed, in this context
there exists
\delta>0
so that for every
x\in X
there are well defined inverse branches

f_{i}^{-1}:B(x,\delta)\to f_{i}^{-1}(B(x,\delta))
,
and the conclusion of Lemma
6.1
holds for every small
0<\varepsilon<\delta
.

In view of the estimates (
6.3
), one can see the frequent hitting times property will
be enough to guarantee that the transition times may grow exponentially fast with the size of the finite pieces
of orbits to be shadowed. This can be observed in the following schematic Figure
6.1
below.

Figure 6.1.
On the left: the image by
f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}
of the ball
B(x_{1},L^{-n_{1}}\varepsilon)
contains a ball of radius
L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon
in green color. On the center: the frequent hitting times property ensures that the image of the green region by a suitable iterate of
f_{0}
covers a ball of
radius
L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon/2

The difficulty now reduces to guarantee that the previous property is still enough to ensure the divergence of the time averages.
For that purpose we will make the following choice of constants.
Let us consider a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 3}
such that

n_{j+1}>2n_{j}+\frac{2\log 2}{\log L}\;\text{for all $j\geqslant 2$}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{k}\big[n_{j}+K(\frac{L^{-2j}\varepsilon}{2})\big]}{n_{k+1}}=0,

(6.4)

and for each
k\geqslant 1
set

z_{k}=\begin{cases}x_{1},\text{\; if $k$ odd}\\
x_{2},\text{\; if $k$ even.}\end{cases}

The following proposition guarantees that there exist points which shadows the finite size orbits of the prescribed points
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, alternately, and that these have divergent time-averages. More precisely:

Proposition 6.3
.

There exists
x\in X
and
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
so that:

(a)

x\in B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)

(b)

for every
k\geqslant 1
there exists
0\leqslant p_{k}\leqslant K(\frac{L^{-2k}\varepsilon}{2})
and a finite
word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}:=\omega^{(k)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(k)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(k)}_{i_{p_{k}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{k}}
such that

f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}(x):=f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}}^{p_{k}}f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(k-1)}}^{p_{k-1}}\dots f_{\kappa}^{n_{2}}f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p_{1}}f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(x)\in B_{f_{\kappa}}(z_{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\quad\forall k\geqslant 2,

(6.5)

where

{\underline{\omega}}=(\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{1}},\omega^{(1)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(1)}_{i_{p_{1}}},\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{2}},\omega^{(2)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(2)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(2)}_{i_{p_{2}}},\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{3}},\ldots).

(6.6)

Moreover,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x)<\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x).

(6.7)

Proof.

Set
B_{1}:=B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)
. By Lemma
6.1
one has that

f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(B_{1})
contains the ball of radius
L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon
around
f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(x_{1})
.
Now, since the semigroup action has frequent hitting times and
L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon<\frac{L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon}{4}
(recall the first inequality in (
6.4
))
there are integers
K_{1}=K(\frac{L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon}{2})\geqslant 1
and
0\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant K_{1}
, a finite word

{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}:=\omega^{(1)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(1)}_{i_{p_{1}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{1}}

and a ball
B_{2}
of radius
\frac{L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon}{2}
so that

B_{2}\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p_{1}}\big(B(f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(x_{1}),L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon)\big)\cap B(x_{2},L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon)\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p_{1}}(B(f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(x_{1}),L^{-2n_{1}}\varepsilon))\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2},n_{2},\varepsilon)

(recall Figure
6.1
). By Lemma
6.1
,

f_{\kappa}^{n_{2}}(B_{2})
contains a ball of radius
\frac{L^{-2n_{2}}\varepsilon}{2}
. Using that
L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon<\frac{L^{-2n_{2}}\varepsilon}{4}
, there exist

K_{2}=K(\frac{L^{-2n_{2}}\varepsilon}{2})\geqslant 1
and
0\leqslant p_{2}\leqslant K_{2}
,
a finite word

{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}:=\omega^{(2)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(2)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(2)}_{i_{p_{2}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{2}}

and a ball
B_{3}

of radius
\frac{L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon}{2}

so that

B_{3}\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}}^{p_{2}}\big(f_{\kappa}^{n_{2}}(B_{2})\big)\,\cap\,B(x_{3},L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon)\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}}^{p_{2}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{2}}(B_{2}))\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{3},n_{3},\varepsilon).

Proceeding recursively, we conclude that for every
s\geqslant 1
there exists
0\leqslant p_{s-1}\leqslant K(\frac{L^{-2n_{s-1}}\varepsilon}{2})
, a finite word

{\underline{\omega}}^{(s)}:=\omega^{(s)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(s)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(s)}_{i_{p_{s}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{s}}

and a ball
B_{s}
of radius
\frac{L^{-n_{s}}\varepsilon}{2}
contained in

f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(s)}}^{p_{s-1}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{s-1}}(B_{s-1}))\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{s},n_{s},\varepsilon)
.
Moreover, if
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
is defined by (
6.6
),
we claim that any point

x\in B_{1}\cap\bigcap_{k>1}\big(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\sum_{j=1}^{k}(n_{j}+p_{j})}\big)^{-1}(B_{k}).

is irregular in the sense of (
6.7
)
(the intersection is non-empty because the right hand-side above consists of an intersection
of compact and nested sets).
Indeed, according to (
6.5
), for each
k\geqslant 1
one has that

y_{k}:=f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}(x)\in B_{f_{\kappa}}(z_{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)
.
In consequence, recalling (
6.1
), (
6.2
) and (
6.4
), if
k\geqslant 1
is even then

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-n_{k+1}\,\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(y_{k}))\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(y_{k}))-n_{k+1}\,\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\psi\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})+n_{k+1}\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}.

In consequence, if
k\geqslant 1
is even and large then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{n_{k+1}}{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}

\displaystyle+\|\psi\|_{\infty}\,\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\big[n_{j}+K(\frac{L^{-2j}\varepsilon}{2})\big]}{n_{k+1}}+\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}

\displaystyle<\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}+\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{3}.

Analogously, it is not hard to check that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(n_{i}+p_{i})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))>\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{3}

for every
k\geqslant 1
odd and large. This implies that the Birkhoff averages
\big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x)\big)_{n\geqslant 1}

do not converge and completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

The crucial point in (
6.4
) is that the constants
K(\cdot)
involved in the proof
may grow arbitrarily fast but are independent of the shadowing points.
For instance, in the special case that
f
is an irrational rotation on the circle, Remark
5.3
ensures that
K(L^{-2n}\varepsilon/2)\simeq 3L^{2n}[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}]\gg n
grows exponentially
fast with
n
.

6.2.
The set of irregular points is Baire generic

The strategy used in the proof of Theorem
C

is slightly different from the classical strategies (see e.g
[
5
,
48
]
), where the use of the specification or the gluing orbit property is used to make successive approximations of points with some preestablished behavior.
The difference does not
rely only on the fact that in the process of shadowing a finite piece of orbit, time lags between the shadowing times
to become larger as these depend on the proximity scale.
In fact, while specification allows to make successive approximations
(z_{k})_{k}
of a certain initial point
x_{0}

by points
z_{k}
whose time averages diverge and so that
\sum_{k\geqslant 1}d(z_{k},x_{0})
is small, the strategy here is to construct nested sequences of sets with a irregular behavior by the analysis of the images of dynamic balls. Such a finer control, provided by the frequent hitting times property, is not imediate
even if the dynamical system satisfies the
specification property. This will become evident in the proof of the following proposition:

Proposition 6.5
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
C
, the set

I_{F}(\varphi_{\psi})
is a Baire residual subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.

The proof of Proposition
6.5
, which occupies the remainder of this subsection, is divided in a three-step construction.
The first two steps, whose content is closer to the arguments presented in Subsection
6.1
, consider non-typical behavior along
paths in the semigroup
G
. In the third step, one constructs nested sets with oscilating behavior and prescribed initial condition for the skew-product.

Consider a strictly decreasing sequence of positive numbers
(\delta_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
tending to zero. Associated to these,
pick a strictly increasing sequence of large positive integers
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
satisfying
(
6.4
) and in such a way that for each
\ell\geqslant 1
the sets

\Gamma_{2\ell-1}:=\Big\{x\in X:\big|\frac{1}{n_{2\ell-1}}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{2\ell-1}-1}\psi(f_{\kappa}^{j}(x))-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}\big|<\delta_{2\ell-1}\Big\}

and

\Gamma_{2\ell}:=\Big\{x\in X:\big|\frac{1}{n_{2\ell}}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{2\ell}-1}\psi(f_{\kappa}^{j}(x))-\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}\big|<\delta_{2\ell}\Big\}

are non-empty. This is possible by ergodicity of the measures
\mu_{i}
with respect to
f_{\kappa}
(
i=1,2
), and the ergodic theorem.

Let
D\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X

be a countable and dense set. Fix
({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
and set

S_{0}=\{({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\}
. Now, for each
\ell\geqslant 1
let
S_{2\ell-1}\subset\Gamma_{2\ell-1}
be a
(f_{\kappa},n_{2\ell-1},8\varepsilon)
-separated set in
X
, i.e. a set so that

\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant n_{2\ell-1}}d(f_{\kappa}^{j}(x),f_{\kappa}^{j}(y))>8\varepsilon

for every distinct
x,y\in S_{2\ell-1}
. Analogously, let
S_{2\ell}\subset\Gamma_{2\ell}
be a
(f_{\kappa},n_{2\ell},8\varepsilon)
-separated set.
Now consider a sequence of positive integers
(N_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
(independent of
{\underline{\omega}}_{0}
and
x_{0}
)
so that

L^{-n_{k+1}}\varepsilon<\min\Big\{\frac{L^{-2^{N_{k}-1}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2N_{k}-1}},L^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\varepsilon/4\Big\}\\
\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;\\
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left[N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}K(\frac{L^{-2^{j}\cdot n_{j}}\varepsilon}{2^{j}})+K(\frac{L^{-2^{N_{i}-1}\cdot n_{i}}\varepsilon}{2^{N_{i}-1}})\right]}{n_{k+1}}=0.

(6.8)

We explain the construction dividing it in steps, which involve the frequent hitting times property.
First we explain the construction of sets which replicate the behavior of the points in
S_{k}
for a
certain number
N_{k}
of times. Then we repeat such argument to construct a nested sequence of sets
points which combine the different behaviors.

1^{st}
Step:

Replication of the behavior of points in each set
S_{k}

Fix
k\geqslant 1
.
For each collection
\underline{x}_{k}=(x_{1}^{k},\cdots,x_{N_{k}}^{k})\in S_{k}^{N_{k}}
we will explore ideas similar the ones used in Proposition
6.3
to construct a set
C(\underline{x}_{k})\subset X
of points which shadow pieces of orbits of points in
S_{k}
. Let us be more precise.
First recall that
B_{1}^{k}:=B(x_{1}^{k},L^{-n_{k}}\varepsilon)\subset B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)
and that Lemma
6.1
ensures

f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B(x_{1}^{k},L^{-n_{k}}\varepsilon))
contains the ball of radius
L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon
around
f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(x_{1}^{k})
.
As the semigroup action has frequent hitting times
there are constants
K_{1}=K(L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon)\geqslant 1
and
0\leqslant p^{k}_{1}\leqslant K_{1}
, a finite word

{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}:=\omega^{(1)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(1)}_{i_{p^{k}_{1}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p^{k}_{1}}

and a ball
B_{2}^{k}
of radius
\frac{L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2}}

so that

\displaystyle B_{2}^{k}

\displaystyle\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p^{k}_{1}}\Big(B(f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(x_{1}^{k}),L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon)\Big)\cap B\Big(x_{2}^{k},\frac{L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p^{k}_{1}}\Big(B(f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(x_{1}),L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon)\Big)\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p^{k}_{1}}\Big(\,f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}\big(B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\big)\,\Big)\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon).

(6.9)

In particular

\displaystyle B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;\big(\,f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p^{k}_{1}}\circ f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}\,\big)^{-1}\Big(\,B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\,\Big)\neq\emptyset.

(6.10)

Using Lemma
6.1
once more, we conclude that

f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B_{2}^{k})\;\text{contains a ball of radius}\;L^{-2n_{k}}\times\frac{L^{-2n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2}}=\frac{L^{-2^{2}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2}}.

Again, using the frequent hitting times property and (
6.9
), there exist
K_{2}=K(\frac{L^{-2^{2}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2}})\geqslant 1
,
0\leqslant p_{2}^{k}\leqslant K_{2}
, a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}:=\omega^{(2)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(2)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(2)}_{i_{p_{2}^{k}}}\in\{0,1,\dots,\kappa\}^{p^{k}_{2}}
and a ball
B_{3}^{k}
of radius
\frac{L^{-2^{2}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{4}}

so that

\displaystyle B_{3}^{k}

\displaystyle\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}}^{p_{2}^{k}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B_{2}^{k}))\cap B(x_{3}^{k},\frac{L^{-2^{2}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{3}})

\displaystyle\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}}^{p_{2}^{k}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}\Big(f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}}^{p^{k}_{1}}\Big(\,f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}\big(B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\big)\,\Big)\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\Big)\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{3}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon).

and its image

f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B_{3}^{k})
contains a ball of radius

\frac{L^{-2^{3}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{4}}
(recall the first choice of constants in (
6.8
)).

Proceeding recursively, for each
3\leqslant i\leqslant N_{k}-1
there exists
a ball
B_{i}^{k}
of radius
\frac{L^{-2^{i-1}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2i-3}}
so that the set
f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B_{i}^{k})

contains a ball of radius

\frac{L^{-2^{i}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(i-1)}}
and there are
0\leqslant p^{k}_{i}\leqslant K_{i}=K(\frac{L^{-2^{i}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(i-1)}})
and
a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(i)}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{i}^{k}}
so that

f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(i)}}^{p_{i}^{k}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{k}}(B_{i}^{k}))\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{i}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)

contains a ball
B_{i+1}^{k}
of radius
\frac{L^{-2^{i}n_{k}}\varepsilon}{2^{2i-1}}
.
For each
\underline{x}_{k}=(x_{1}^{k}\cdots x_{N_{k}}^{k})\in S_{k}^{N_{k}}
we collect the data

\displaystyle\big(\,n_{k}\,;\,({\underline{\omega}}^{(1)},p^{k}_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}^{(N_{k}-1)},p^{k}_{N_{k}-1})\,;\,(B_{1}^{k},\dots,B_{N_{k}}^{k})\,\big)

(6.11)

given by the previous algorithm, where the times
p^{k}_{i}
(depending both on the size of the orbits
and the points to be shadowed) are bounded by
K_{i}
.
Moreover, by construction, the set

\displaystyle C(\underline{x}_{k}):=B_{1}^{k}(x^{k}_{1})\cap\bigcap_{i=2}^{N_{k}-1}\left(f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{k}+\,p_{j}^{k})}\right)^{-1}\big(B^{k}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})\big)

(6.12)

is non-empty, where
{\underline{\omega}}={\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}(\underline{x}_{k})
is defined by

\displaystyle{\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}(\underline{x}_{k}):=(\,\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{k}\,\text{times}},\underbrace{\omega^{(1)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(1)}_{i_{p_{1}}}}_{=\,\underline{\omega}^{(1)}},\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{k}\,\text{times}},\ldots,\underbrace{\omega^{(k-1)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(k-1)}_{i_{1}}\,\dots\,\omega^{(k-1)}_{i_{p_{k-1}}}}_{{}_{=\,\underline{\omega}^{(N_{k}-1)}}},\underbrace{\kappa,\kappa,\dots\kappa}_{n_{k}\,\text{times}}\,).

(6.13)

For future reference we denote by
B^{k}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
the balls
B^{k}_{i}
as before, to emphasize that the latter are contained
in a neighborhood of the point
x_{i}^{k}
. Moreover, any point
x\in C(\underline{x}_{k})

satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x\in B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon)\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\\
f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{k}+\,p_{j}^{k})}(x)\in B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{i}^{k},n_{k},\varepsilon),\quad\forall\,1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{k}.\end{cases}

2^{nd}
Step:

Construction of nested sets with oscilating behavior

In this step we explain the construction of nested sets which shadow points
in successive collections
S_{k}^{N_{k}}
. We first make explicit such construction involving collections
of points in
S_{1}^{N_{1}}
and
S_{2}^{N_{2}}
.

Given
\underline{x}_{1}=(x_{1}^{1},\cdots,x_{N_{1}}^{1})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}
, let
C(\underline{x}_{1})
be the set
defined by (
6.12
) and consider the ball

B_{N_{1}}^{1}(x_{N_{1}}^{1})\subset B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{N_{1}}^{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)
be the ball
of radius
\frac{L^{-2^{N_{1}-1}n_{1}}\varepsilon}{2^{2N_{1}-3}}

whose image
f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(B_{N_{1}}^{1}(x_{N_{1}}^{1}))
contains a ball of radius
\frac{L^{-2^{N_{1}-1}n_{1}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(N_{1}-1)}}
.
By choice of the constants (recall (
6.8
)) we have
L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}\frac{L^{-2^{N_{1}-1}n_{1}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(N_{1}-1)}}
.
Hence, the frequent hitting times property implies that there exists
0\leqslant P_{1}\leqslant K(\frac{L^{-2^{N_{1}-1}n_{1}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(N_{1}-1)}})
, a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}:=\omega^{(12)}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(12)}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(12)}_{i_{P_{1}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{P_{1}}
and ball
B_{1}^{2}
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon
so that

B_{1}^{2}\subset f_{\underline{\omega}^{(12)}}^{P_{1}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(B_{N_{1}}^{1}))\cap B(x_{2}^{1},L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon)\subset f_{\underline{\omega}^{(12)}}^{P_{1}}(f_{\kappa}^{n_{1}}(B_{N_{1}}^{1}))\cap B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{2}^{1},n_{2},\varepsilon).

Then, we can apply the argument used in the
1^{st}
Step recursively
(noting that when
k=2
the only difference is that the ball
B_{1}^{2}
is half the radius of the corresponding
object in the
1^{st}
Step) and deduce that, in addition to the data in (
6.11
),
for each
2\leqslant\ell\leqslant k
there exists an integer

0\leqslant P_{\ell}\leqslant K(\frac{L^{-2^{N_{i}-1}n_{i}}\varepsilon}{2^{2(N_{i}-1)}})
, a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(\ell(\ell+1))}:=\omega^{(\ell(\ell+1))}_{i_{0}}\omega^{(\ell(\ell+1))}_{i_{1}}\dots\omega^{(\ell(\ell+1))}_{i_{P_{\ell}}}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{P_{\ell}}

so that the set

\displaystyle C(\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{2},\dots,\underline{x}_{k}):=

\displaystyle B_{1}^{1}(x^{1}_{1})\;\cap\;\bigcap_{i=2}^{N_{1}-1}\left(f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}+\,p_{j}^{k})}\right)^{-1}\big(B^{1}_{i}(x_{i}^{1})\big)

\displaystyle\;\cap\;\bigcap_{\ell=2}^{k}\bigcap_{i=2}^{N_{\ell}-1}\left(f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{s=1}^{\ell-1}P_{s}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}+\,p_{j}^{\ell})}\right)^{-1}\big(B^{\ell}_{i}(x_{i}^{\ell})\big)

is non-empty, where

{\underline{\omega}}=(\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}(\underline{x}_{1}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}(\underline{x}_{2}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},\,\dots,\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}(\underline{x}_{k})\,)

It is immediate from the construction that

C(\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{2},\dots,\underline{x}_{k},\underline{x}_{k+1})\subset C(\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{2},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})

for every
k\geqslant 1
and any collections
\underline{x}_{i}\in S_{i}^{N_{i}}
.

3^{rd}
Step:

Construction of nested sets in
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
with oscilating behavior and prescribed initial condition for the skew-product map

We first explain how to construct points
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
whose orbit by
F
starts close to the point
({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in S_{0}
and, subsequently,
its projection
\pi_{X}(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))
over
X
shadows the finite pieces of orbits of any chosen collection
of points
\underline{x}_{1}=(x_{1}^{1},\cdots,x_{N_{1}}^{1})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}
. Let us be more precise.

Considering the product metric on
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
, the ball
B(({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}),\varepsilon)

of radius
\varepsilon
in
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
coincides with the product of the cylinder
[{\underline{\omega}}_{0}]_{[\lfloor\log\varepsilon\rfloor,\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}

by the ball
B(x_{0},\varepsilon)
. For that reason, in order to consider non-typical points for the skew-product it is natural to proceed in the construction
of consider the set
f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{0}}^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}(B(x_{0},\varepsilon))\subset X
, which contains the ball of radius
L^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\varepsilon
around

f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{0}}^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}(x_{0})
.
Fix a collection
\underline{x}_{1}=(x_{1}^{1},\cdots,x_{N_{1}}^{1})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}
and observe that
L^{-n_{1}}\varepsilon<L^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\varepsilon/4
(recall (
6.8
)).
Then, the frequent hitting times property implies that there exists
0\leqslant P_{0}\leqslant K(\varepsilon)

and a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(01)}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{P_{0}}
so that the image

f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(01)}}^{P_{0}}(B(f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{0}}^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}(x_{0}),L^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\varepsilon))
contains a ball
B_{1}^{1}\subset B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)
of radius

L^{-n_{1}}\varepsilon/2
. Proceeding
ipsis literis
as in the
2^{nd}
Step (with
B_{1}^{1}
as above) we conclude that
the modified sets

\displaystyle C(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})=B(x_{0},\varepsilon)\cap\big(f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{01}}^{P_{0}}f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{0}}^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\big)^{-1}\Big(C(\underline{x}_{1},\underline{x}_{2},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})\Big)

(6.14)

are non-empty and nested, as desired. Even though we omit the dependence of the sets
C(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})
on the finite word

\displaystyle{\underline{\omega}}=(\,[{\underline{\omega}}_{0}]_{[\lfloor\log\varepsilon\rfloor,\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]},\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(01)},{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}(\underline{x}_{1}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}(\underline{x}_{2}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},\,\dots,\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(k)}(\underline{x}_{k})\,)

(6.15)

depending on
(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})
,
used to determine it, we need to bookkeep it. Here, by some abuse of notation we are denoting by
[{\underline{\omega}}_{0}]_{[\lfloor\log\varepsilon\rfloor,\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}

the finite word that defines the cylinder set.

Now we are in a position to resume the proof of Proposition
6.5
and to construct a
G_{\delta}
-dense subset of points with non-typical
behavior for the skew-product. Take the non-empty compact set

\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))=\bigcup\;\;[{\underline{\omega}}]\times\overline{C(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})}

where the union is taken among all possibilities
(\underline{x}_{1}\cdots\underline{x}_{k})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}\times...\times S_{k}^{N_{k}}
and

{\underline{\omega}}={\underline{\omega}}(x_{0},\underline{x}_{1}\cdots\underline{x}_{k})
is the finite word determined by (
6.15
).
Define

\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})):=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{j=\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\rfloor}^{\infty}\bigcup_{({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D}\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\big(\frac{1}{j},({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\big),

where
\varepsilon_{0}
was chosen in (
6.2
).
By construction the sets in (
6.14
) are nested as well as the cylinders of increasing size in
\Sigma_{\kappa}
, and so

\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k+1}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))\subseteq\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))
for every point

({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
,
\varepsilon>0
and
k\geqslant 1
.
We consider, alternatively, the family

\mathfrak{F}_{k}(\varepsilon)=\bigcup\;\;[{\underline{\omega}}]\times\overline{C(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})}

where the union is taken among all possible elements
({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
and
(\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}\times...\times S_{k}^{N_{k}}
,
and the finite words
{\underline{\omega}}
are once more determined by (
6.15
).
In this case, define

\mathfrak{F}_{k}=\bigcup_{j=\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\rfloor}^{\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{k}\big(\frac{1}{j}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{F}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{F}_{k}

By construction,
\mathfrak{F}_{k+1}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{k}
for every
k\geqslant 1
and
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{F}
.
The proof of the proposition is now reduced to the proof of the following two lemmas.

Lemma 6.6
.

\mathfrak{F}
is a
G_{\delta}
-dense subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.

Proof.

We claim that

\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
is dense in
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.
Indeed, for
r>0
and
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
there
exist
j\geqslant 1
and
({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
such that
d_{\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X}(({\underline{\omega}},x),({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))<1/j<r/2
. Any point

(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},y)\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}(1/j,({\underline{\omega}},x_{0}))
satisfies

d_{\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X}((\tilde{\underline{\omega}},y),({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))<1/j
and, consequently,
d_{\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X}((\tilde{\underline{\omega}},y),({\underline{\omega}},x))<2/j<r
.
As
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{F}
, this shows that
\mathfrak{F}
is dense in
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
as well.

Now, in order to prove that
\mathfrak{F}
is a
G_{\delta}
-set,
we define the sets

\mathfrak{G}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})):=\bigcup\big\{\;[{\underline{\omega}}]\times C(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k}):({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D,\;(\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}\times...\times S_{k}^{N_{k}}\big\},

with the finite words
{\underline{\omega}}
given as in (
6.15
).
Note that
\mathfrak{G}_{k+1}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))\subset\mathfrak{G}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))
and

\mathfrak{G}_{k}(\varepsilon,x_{0})\subset\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))
for every
k\geqslant 1
,
\varepsilon>0
and
({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
.
We also observe that
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k+1}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))\subset\mathfrak{G}_{k}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))
for every
k\geqslant 1
. In fact, given

(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},z)\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{k+1}(\varepsilon,({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))
, there exists a
k
-uple
(\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k+1})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}\times...\times S_{k+1}^{N_{k+1}}

and a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}={\underline{\omega}}(x_{0},\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k+1})\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{N}}
determined by (
6.15
) in such a way that

(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},z)\in[{\underline{\omega}}]\times\overline{C(x_{0}\,\underline{x}_{1}\cdots\underline{x}_{k+1})}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}(z)\in\overline{B_{N_{j}}^{j}},\;\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant k+1,

where
B_{N_{j}}^{j}
is a ball contained in
B_{f_{\kappa}}(x^{j}_{N_{j}},n_{j},\varepsilon)
as described above (one can replace
{\underline{\omega}}
by
\tilde{\underline{\omega}}
in the second
expression above because the cylinder
[{\underline{\omega}}]
determines
{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}
positive coordinates of an element
in
\Sigma_{\kappa}
). Furthermore, it is immediate from the construction that

f_{\tilde{\underline{\omega}}}^{-(N_{k+1}n_{k+1}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k+1}-1}p^{k}_{j}+P_{k})}(\overline{B}_{N_{k+1}}^{k+1})\subset B_{N_{k}}^{k}.

Thus

f_{\tilde{\underline{\omega}}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}(z)\in B_{N_{k}}^{k},

which proves that
(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},z)\in\mathfrak{G}_{k}
.
Altogether, we conclude that

\mathfrak{F}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\;\Big[\bigcup_{j=\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\rfloor}^{\infty}\bigcup_{({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D}\mathfrak{G}_{k}(\frac{1}{j},({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0}))\Big]

is obtainable as countable intersection of open sets, hence it is a
G_{\delta}
-set. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Finally, in order to complete the proof of Proposition
6.5
it remains to prove that all points in
\mathfrak{F}
are Birkhoff irregular
for the skew-product
F
and the continuous observable
\varphi_{\psi}:\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X\to\mathbb{R}
. More precisely:

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\mathfrak{F}
it holds that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x))<\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x)).

Proof.

By construction, for each
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\mathfrak{F}
and
k\geqslant 1
there are
j\geqslant 1
,

({\underline{\omega}}_{0},x_{0})\in D
and
(\,\underline{x}_{1},\dots,\underline{x}_{k})\in S_{1}^{N_{1}}\times...\times S_{k}^{N_{k}}

so that the first entries of the word
{\underline{\omega}}
are of the form

(\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(1)}(\underline{x}_{1}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(2)}(\underline{x}_{2}),\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},\,\dots,\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(kk+1)}\,)

and

f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{(\ell n_{i}+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell-1}p_{s}^{j}+P_{i-1})+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(N_{j}n_{j}+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{j}-1}p_{s}^{j}+P_{j-1})}(x)\in\overline{B^{i}_{\ell}}\qquad\;\forall 1\leqslant\ell\leqslant N_{i}\quad\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant k,

where

B^{i}_{\ell}
is a ball contained in the dynamic ball
B_{f_{\kappa}}(x^{i}_{\ell},n_{i},\frac{1}{j})

as described in the previous subsection.
In particular, for each
k\geqslant 1
the iterate
y_{k}:=f_{\underline{\omega}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}(x)
belongs to
B_{f_{\kappa}}(x_{1}^{k+1},n_{k+1},\frac{1}{j})
.
By triangular inequality and the choice of constants in (
6.2
) and (
6.8
), if
k\geqslant 1
is even and large then

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}

\displaystyle\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-n_{k+1}\,\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}}\psi(f_{\kappa}^{j}(y_{k}))\Big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k+1}}\psi(f_{\kappa}^{j}(y_{k}))-n_{k+1}\,\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\psi\|_{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})

\displaystyle{+n_{k+1}\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}}

\displaystyle<\Big[\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}+\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{3}\Big]\;\Big(n_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})\Big).

Analogously, we get that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}\sum_{j=0}^{n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(N_{i}n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}-1}p_{j}^{i}+P_{i-1})}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))>\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\frac{\int\psi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\psi\,d\mu_{1}}{3}

for every large and odd integer
k\geqslant 1
. This implies that the Birkhoff averages
\big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x)\big)_{n\geqslant 1}

do not converge, proving the lemma.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
C

The proof of the theorem is now a direct consequence of the results presented in the previous sections. Indeed,
one knows that
I_{F}(\varphi_{\psi})
is a Baire generic subset of
\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X
. Recalling that

\pi:\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X\to\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
denotes the projection on the first coordinate,
it follows from the Kuratowski-Ulam theorem (cf.
[
46
]
) that the set

\Big\{{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon I_{F}(\varphi_{\psi})\cap\pi^{-1}({\underline{\omega}})\;\text{is a Baire residual subset of}\,\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times X\Big\}

is a Baire residual subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}
. As
I_{F}(\varphi_{\psi})\cap\pi^{-1}({\underline{\omega}})=\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times I_{\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}}}(\psi)
, this proves the
first statement in the theorem.
The second one is a direct consequence of the inclusion (
2.4
) and the first statement.
This completes the proof of the theorem.

7.
Topological complexity on the skew-product

In this section we prove Theorem
D
.

7.1.
Entropy estimates obtained from ergodic measures

Let
X
be a compact metric space,
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
be a collection of bi-Lipschitz
homeomorphisms on
X
,
\sigma:\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the full shift and
F:\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X\to\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X
be the skew product

F({\underline{\omega}},x)=(\sigma({\underline{\omega}}),f_{\omega_{0}}(x)),\quad\text{for every}\;({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X.

Assume that the semigroup action generated by
G_{1}
has frequent hitting times. In rough terms,
this ensures that
F
has frequent hitting times along the fibers. Let

\varphi:\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous observable.

Assume that
h_{*}(\varphi)
, given by (
2.5
), is strictly positive (otherwise there is nothing to prove).
Given an arbitrary and fixed
\gamma\in(0,1)
, let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be
F
-invariant and ergodic probabilities
such that
\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}
and
h_{\mu_{i}}(F)>h_{*}(\varphi)-\gamma
.
Now, consider a strictly decreasing sequence of positive numbers
(\delta_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
tending to zero so that

\delta_{1}<\frac{1}{4}(\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1})

and, associated to these, pick a strictly increasing sequence of large positive integers

(\mathfrak{n}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
so that,
for each
\ell\geqslant 1
, the set

\Gamma_{2\ell-1}:=\Big\{({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell-1},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell-1}\Big\}

has
\mu_{1}
-measure larger than
\frac{1}{2}
