and

\Gamma_{2\ell}:=\Big\{({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell}\Big\}

has
\mu_{2}
-measure larger than
\frac{1}{2}
.
This is possible by ergodicity of the measures

\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
together with the ergodic theorem and Lusin’s theorem.
By uniform continuity of
\varphi
and Katok’s entropy formula (recall Subsection
4
),
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle|\varphi(\omega,x)-\varphi(\omega^{\prime},x^{\prime})|<\frac{\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}

(7.1)

for every
(\omega,x),(\omega^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X
so that
d((\omega,x),(\omega^{\prime},x^{\prime}))<4\varepsilon

and, for each
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\underline{h}_{\mu_{i}}(F,8\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})=\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log N^{\mu_{i}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})>h_{*}(\varphi)-2\gamma.

(7.2)

This constant
\varepsilon>0
will be fixed throughout the remainder of the argument.

The next lemma allows one to select a special class of dynamically separated points inside each of the sets
\Gamma_{\ell}
.
More precisely:

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists a sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
of positive integers
such that:

(1)

n_{\ell}\geqslant\max\{\;\mathfrak{n}_{\ell},\;(\ell+2)\cdot n_{\ell-1}+\Big[\frac{2\log 2}{\log L}{+\log\varepsilon^{-1}}\Big],\;\ell\cdot K(L^{-2n_{\ell-1}}\,L^{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}}\,2\varepsilon)\;\}
for every
\ell\geqslant 2

(2)

there exists an
(n_{\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\subset\Gamma_{\ell}
(with respect to
F
)
of cardinality

\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\geqslant\exp(\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)n_{\ell}\,)\quad\text{for every $\ell\geqslant 1$.}

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\ell
. Set
\ell=1
. Since
\mu_{1}(\Gamma_{1})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
,
inequality (
7.2
) implies that the minimum cardinality of a
(n,8\varepsilon)
-generating set
for
\Gamma_{1}
is bounded below by
N^{\mu_{i}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})
. Pick
n_{1}\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{1}

so that

N^{\mu_{1}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})\geqslant\exp(\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)n\,),\quad\forall n\geqslant n_{1}.

Recall that any
(n,8\varepsilon)
-generating set is a
(n,4\varepsilon)
-separated set. Hence, we deduce that there exists an

(n_{1},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{1}\subset\Gamma_{1}
with cardinality

\#\mathcal{S}_{1}\geqslant e^{\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)n_{1}\,}
.

Now, assume that the lemma is proven for every
1\leqslant\ell<k
. If
\ell=k
then pick a large integer

n_{\ell}\geqslant\max\{\;\mathfrak{n}_{\ell},\;(\ell+2)\cdot n_{\ell-1}+\Big[\frac{2\log 2}{\log L}{+\log\varepsilon^{-1}}\Big],\;\ell\cdot K(L^{-2n_{\ell-1}}\,L^{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}}\,2\varepsilon)\;\}

so that

N^{\mu_{\ell({\tiny\mbox{mod}}1)}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,\frac{1}{2})\geqslant\exp(\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)n\,),\quad\forall n\geqslant n_{\ell}.

Then, taking
n=n_{\ell}
above we conclude that
that there exists an
(n_{\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\subset\Gamma_{\ell}
with cardinality

\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\geqslant e^{\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)n_{\ell}\,}
, as claimed. This proves the induction assumption and completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

By construction, the sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
is strictly increasing and it satisfies

\lim_{\ell\to+\infty}\frac{n_{\ell-1}}{n_{\ell}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\ell\to+\infty}\frac{K(L^{-2n_{\ell-1}}\,L^{\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}}\,2\varepsilon)}{n_{\ell}}=0.

In particular this ensures that
The second expression reflects that the size of the orbits in
\Gamma_{\ell}
are much larger than the largest possible transition time
necessary for shadowing pieces of orbits in
\Gamma_{\ell-1}
and
\Gamma_{\ell}
consecutively.

7.2.
A Moran set of irregular points in
\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X
with large entropy

The main goal of this subsection is the construction of a Moran set, contained in the set
I_{F}(\varphi)

of irregular in
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
, having large upper topological entropy.

1^{st}
Step:

Understanding dynamic balls for points in
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}

Fix
\ell\geqslant 1
,
and let
B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),n_{\ell},\varepsilon)\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
be the dynamic ball with respect to
F
, centered at
({\underline{\omega}},x)
, of radius
\varepsilon
and
length
n_{\ell}
.
Given
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
and integers
m\leqslant n
we denote by

{\underline{\omega}}_{[m,n]}:=\big\{\,\tilde{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon{\underline{\omega}}_{j}=\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}_{j},\,\forall j\in[m,n]\,\big\}

the cylinder set determined by the infinite word
{\underline{\omega}}
at the positions in the interval
[m,n]
.
We need the following auxiliary lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
,
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
and
\ell\geqslant 1
one has that

B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),n_{\ell},\varepsilon)=B_{\sigma}({\underline{\omega}},n_{\ell},\varepsilon)\times B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n_{\ell},\varepsilon)

where

B_{\sigma}({\underline{\omega}},n_{\ell},\varepsilon)

is the cylinder set
{\underline{\omega}}_{\,[-\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor\,,\,n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}

and

B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n_{\ell},\varepsilon):=\big\{y\in X\colon d_{X}(g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x),g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(y))<\varepsilon\,\text{ for every }\,0\leqslant j\leqslant n_{\ell}\big\}.

In particular,

(1)

F^{n_{\ell}}\big(\,B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),n_{\ell},\varepsilon)\,\big)\supseteq\sigma^{n_{\ell}}\big({\underline{\omega}}_{\,[-n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor,\,\lfloor-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}\big)\times B(f^{n_{\ell}}_{\underline{\omega}}(x),L^{-2n_{\ell}}\varepsilon);

(2)

F^{n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\big(\,B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),n_{\ell},\varepsilon)\,\big)

\supseteq\sigma^{n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\big({\underline{\omega}}_{\,[-n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor,\,\lfloor-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}\big)\times B(f^{n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}_{\underline{\omega}}(x),L^{-(2{n_{\ell}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)

Proof.

The first claim is an immediate consequence of the fact that the step skew-products are constant on cylinders of size one in the shift, and that we are taking the maximum metric.
Then, item (
1
) follows from the definition and Lemma
6.1
, which ensures that

f_{\omega}^{n}(B_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x,n,\varepsilon))
contains the ball of radius
L^{-2n}\varepsilon
around
f_{\omega}^{n}(x)
.
Finally, the inclusion in item (
2
) is obtained from item (
1
) using the same argument as above and
iteration by
F^{\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}
.
∎

Comparing the expressions appearing in items (
1
) and (
2
) in the lemma,
we conclude that any point
(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},\tilde{x})
which
\varepsilon
-shadows the piece of orbit of a point
({\underline{\omega}},x)
during
n_{\ell}

iterates is such
\sigma^{n}(\tilde{\underline{\omega}})
belongs to a cylinder of deepth
\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor
. As a consequence, one needs to make
\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor
further iterations to drop this dependence (cf. item (
2
)
in Lemma
7.2
above).

2^{nd}
Step:

Construction of a Moran set of points with oscilating behavior

Given
({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
({\underline{\omega}}_{2},x_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
, Lemma
7.2
(
2
) implies that

F^{n_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\big(\,B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x),n_{1},\varepsilon)\,\big)

contains the set

\sigma^{n_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\big({\underline{\omega}}_{\,[-\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor\,,\,n_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor]}\big)\times B(f^{n_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}_{\underline{\omega}}(x),L^{-(2{n_{1}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon).

On the other hand,
B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(x_{2},n_{2},\varepsilon)
contains the ball centered at
x_{2}
with radius
L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon
.
Then, the frequent hitting times property along the fiber of
F
ensures that there exists

0\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant K(L^{-(2{n_{1}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)
and a finite word

{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p}_{1}

so that

f^{p_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}(B(f^{n_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}_{\underline{\omega}}(x),L^{-(2{n_{1}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon))\cap B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(x_{2},n_{2},\varepsilon)

contains a ball
B_{1}^{2}
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon
.
Thus

\displaystyle C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),({\underline{\omega}}_{2},x_{2})\,)

\displaystyle:={{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{1}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\;\cap\;F^{-(p_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+n_{1})}\,\Big({{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{2}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{1}^{2}\Big)

is a compact and non-empty subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.
Observe now that
f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}^{n_{2}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}(B_{1}^{2})
contains a ball of radius

\frac{1}{2}L^{-(2n_{2}+\lfloor-\log(\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor))}\varepsilon\geqslant L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon
.
Thus, if
({\underline{\omega}}_{3},x_{3})\in\mathcal{S}_{3}
, repeating the argument,
there exists
0\leqslant p_{2}\leqslant K(L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon)
,
a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{2}}
and a ball

B_{2}^{3}\subset B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{3}}(x_{3},n_{3},\varepsilon)
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{3}}\varepsilon

such that

\displaystyle C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),({\underline{\omega}}_{2},x_{2}),({\underline{\omega}}_{3},x_{3})\,)

\displaystyle:={{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{1}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\;\cap\;F^{-(p_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+n_{1})}\,\Big({{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{2}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{1}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\;\cap\;F^{-\sum_{i=1}^{2}(p_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+n_{i})}\,\Big({{\underline{\omega}}_{3}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{3}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{2}^{3}\Big)

is non-empty subset of
C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),({\underline{\omega}}_{2},x_{2})\,)
.
More generally, given
k\geqslant 1
and a
k
-uple
(({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k}))\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}
, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
there exist

0\leqslant p_{i}\leqslant K(L^{-n_{i}}\varepsilon)
, a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(i(i+1))}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{i}}

and a ball
B_{i}^{i+1}\subset B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i+1}}(x_{i+1},n_{i+1},\varepsilon)
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{i+1}}\varepsilon
so that

\displaystyle C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)

\displaystyle:={{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{1}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\;\cap\;F^{-(p_{1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+n_{1})}\,\Big({{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{2}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{1}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\dots

\displaystyle\;\cap\;F^{-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(p_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+n_{i})}\,\Big({{\underline{\omega}}_{k}}_{\,[-\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\,,\,n_{k}+\log(\varepsilon^{-1})]}\times B_{k-1}^{k}\Big)

is non-empty. By construction, the previous family of cylinder sets satisfy

C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k}),({\underline{\omega}}_{k+1},x_{k+1})\,)\subset C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)

for every

(({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k}),({\underline{\omega}}_{k+1},x_{k+1}))\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}\times\mathcal{S}_{k+1}
and
k\geqslant 1
.

For the sake of completeness, let us emphasize that the transition times
p_{i}
are functions of the collections

(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k}))
. Moreover, since transition times are not constant, one cannot ensure that points
in different cylinder sets of

\mathcal{\tilde{C}}_{k}:=\big\{C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)\colon(({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k}))\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}\big\}

are separated by some suitable iterate of
F
. We proceed to show this is the case, at least for a relevant proportion of cylinder sets.
Let us be more precise.
For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
consider the
i^{\text{th}}
-transition time function

\begin{array}[]{rccc}p_{i}:&\mathcal{\tilde{C}}_{k}&\to&\{0,1,\dots,K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)\}.\end{array}

By the pigeonhole principle, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there exists

0\leqslant t_{i}\leqslant K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)
and a collection
\mathcal{C}_{k}\subset\mathcal{\tilde{C}}_{k}
such that

\#{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\geqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{2}\,\#\mathcal{S}_{1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\,K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)}

(7.3)

and
p_{i}(C)=t_{i}
for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

For each
k\geqslant 1
let the subcollection
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
and the integers

(t_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1}
be given as above. There exists
K_{0}>0

such that:

(1)

for any distinct
C_{1},C_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{k}
,
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in C_{1}
and
(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},\tilde{x})\in C_{2}

it holds that
d_{\alpha_{k}}(({\underline{\omega}},x),(\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}},\tilde{x}))>\varepsilon
,

(2)

\#{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\geqslant K_{0}\,e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\,\alpha_{k}}

where
\alpha_{k}=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+t_{i})
.

Proof.

Fix
k\geqslant 1
. For any distinct
k
-uples

C_{1}=\tilde{C}(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)

and
C_{2}=\tilde{C}(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\tilde{x}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{\underline{\omega}}_{k},\tilde{x}_{k})\,)

belonging to
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{k}

there exists
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
so that
({\underline{\omega}}_{j},x_{j})\neq(\tilde{\underline{\omega}}_{j},\tilde{x}_{j})
.
Thus, if
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in C_{1}
and
(\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}},\tilde{x})\in C_{2}
then

\displaystyle d(F^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})}({\underline{\omega}},x),

\displaystyle F^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})}(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},\tilde{x}))

\displaystyle\geqslant d(({\underline{\omega}}_{j},x_{j}),(\tilde{\underline{\omega}}_{j},\tilde{x}_{j}))-d(F^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})}({\underline{\omega}},x),({\underline{\omega}}_{j},x_{j}))

\displaystyle-d(F^{\sum_{i=1}^{j}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})}(\tilde{\underline{\omega}},\tilde{x})),(\tilde{\underline{\omega}}_{j},\tilde{x}_{j}))>\varepsilon,

thus proving the first item in the lemma.
The proof of the second item follows from quantitative information concerning (
7.3
). Indeed, using Lemma
7.1
(1)
one has that
{n_{i+1}}\geqslant{K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)}
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{i+1}}\;\;e^{-(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)\,[\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)]}\geqslant e^{-\gamma[n_{i+1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+t_{i}]}

for every large
i\geqslant 1
.
Together with Lemma
7.1
(2), this ensures that there exists
K_{0}>0
so that

\displaystyle\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{2}\,\#\mathcal{S}_{1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\,K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\geqslant e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)\,[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{n_{i+1}}\,\frac{n_{i+1}}{K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{n_{i+1}}\;e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)\,[n_{i+1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+t_{i}]}\;e^{-(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)\,[\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor+K(L^{-(2{n_{i}}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor)}\varepsilon)]}

\displaystyle\geqslant K_{0}\,e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\,\alpha_{k}}

for every
k\geqslant 1
,
as claimed.
∎

Now, consider the Moran set
\mathfrak{F}
, obtained as a countable intersection of
compact and nested subsets, defined by

\mathfrak{F}:=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{F}_{k}

where

\mathfrak{F}_{k}:=\bigcup\Big\{\overline{C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)}:C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{k},x_{k})\,)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\Big\}.

We now observe that the compact and non-empty set
\mathfrak{F}
is contained in
I_{F}(\varphi)
.

Lemma 7.4
.

For any
({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\mathfrak{F}
we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))<\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x)).

Proof.

This proof is identical to the proof of Lemma
6.7
, and for that reason we shall omit it.
∎

3^{rd}
Step:

Estimating the topological entropy of
\mathfrak{F}

In order to estimate the topological entropy of the Moran set
\mathfrak{F}
for the skew-product
F
we will make use of the generalized
entropy distribution principle stated as Theorem
4.1
. For that, we define a special sequence of probability measures
\mu_{k}

supported in points selected among the elements of sets in the family
\mathcal{C}_{k}
as follows:
for each
C\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
choose a point
z_{C}\in C

and consider
the probability measure

\mu_{k}=\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k}}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k}}\delta_{z_{C}}.

Lemma 7.5
.

If
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{k}=\mu
(in the weak
∗
-topology) then
\mu(\mathfrak{F})=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu_{k}=\mu
in the weak
∗
-topology. For each
\ell>0
and
i>0
we have
\mu_{\ell+i}(\mathfrak{F}_{\ell+i})=1
and as

\mathfrak{F}_{\ell+i}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\ell}
then
\mu_{\ell+i}(\mathfrak{F}_{\ell})=1
. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{\ell}
is a closed set, we have

\mu(\mathfrak{F}_{\ell})\geqslant\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{F}_{\ell})=1
. Therefore,
\mu(\mathfrak{F})=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mu(\mathfrak{F}_{\ell})=1
.
∎

In order to apply the generalized entropy distribution principle, one needs to estimate the measure of dynamic balls intersecting the Moran set.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be small and
\mathbf{n}\geqslant 1
be fixed and large, satisfying

n_{j}\geqslant j\cdot K(L^{-2n_{j-1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\,2\varepsilon)>\frac{1}{\gamma}\log K(L^{-2n_{j-1}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\,2\varepsilon)

(7.4)

for every
j\geqslant\mathbf{n}
(recall Lemma
7.1
).
Pick also the unique
\ell=\ell(\mathbf{n})\geqslant 1
so that

\alpha_{\ell-1}\leqslant\mathbf{n}<\alpha_{\ell}
, where the sequence
\alpha_{\ell}
was defined in Lemma
7.3
.
Consider an arbitrary dynamic ball
B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X

which intersects the set
\mathfrak{F}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

\mu_{k+\ell}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))\leqslant e^{-\mathbf{n}(h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma)}
for every large
k\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

If
\mu_{k+\ell}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))=0
there is nothing to prove. Otherwise
\mu_{k+\ell}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))>0
and, using that
the probability
\mu_{k+\ell}
is an atomic probability supported on points of
\mathfrak{F}_{k+\ell}
, we conclude
that
\mathfrak{F}_{k+\ell}\cap B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
.
We claim that all points in such intersection
belong to a single fixed cylinder
C_{*}=C(\,({\underline{\omega}}_{1},x_{1}),\dots,({\underline{\omega}}_{\ell},x_{\ell})\,)\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
.
In fact, if
({\underline{\omega}}^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\cap C^{\prime}
,
(\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}},\tilde{x})\in B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\cap\tilde{C}
with
C^{\prime},\tilde{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
and
C^{\prime}\neq C
then item
(1)
in Lemma
7.3
guarantees that
d_{\alpha_{\ell}}(({\underline{\omega}},x),(\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}},\tilde{x}))>\varepsilon
. As
\alpha_{\ell}>\mathbf{n}
this leads to a contradiction with the fact that both points belong to the same dynamic ball of radius
\varepsilon
and length
\mathbf{n}
.

These estimates show that the number of cylinder sets constructed at each step (which involve the selection of those having the same transition times) are close to the exponential rate provided by entropy, a fact which imposes constraints of size on the transition times in comparison to the finite orbit size. Let us be more precise.
By construction and inequality (
7.4
), for each fixed
C_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
one has

\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell+1}\colon C\cap C_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}\geqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell+1}}{K(L^{-2n_{\ell}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor}\,2\varepsilon)}>e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-3\gamma)\,n_{\ell+1}}\,e^{-\gamma\,n_{\ell+1}}

provided that
\ell
is large (which means large
\mathbf{n}
). Using the same argument recursively we conclude that

\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}>e^{(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}

for every large
k
. Thus

\displaystyle\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{*}\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}{\min\limits_{C_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\;\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}}

\displaystyle<e^{-(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}\;\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}.

(7.5)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mu_{k+\ell}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\\
C\cap C_{*}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\delta_{z_{C}}\left(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\displaystyle\frac{\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{*}\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}<e^{-(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}.

Notice that

\displaystyle(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\cdot\sum_{i=k+1}^{k+\ell}n_{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\Big[n_{k+\ell}+\sum_{i=1}^{k+\ell-1}(n_{i}+\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{k+\ell-1}(\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})\Big]

\displaystyle=(h_{*}(\varphi)-4\gamma)\Big[\alpha_{k+\ell}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{k+\ell-1}(\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}\rfloor+t_{i})}{\alpha_{k+\ell}}\Big]

\displaystyle\geqslant(h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma)\,\alpha_{k+\ell}\geqslant(h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma)\,\mathbf{n}

for every large
k\geqslant 1
. Altogether this ultimately implies that

\mu_{k+\ell}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))\leqslant e^{-\mathbf{n}\,(h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma)\,}
, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the theorem. Indeed, on the one hand any accumulation point of
(\mu_{k})_{k}
is supported on
\mathfrak{F}
, by Lemma
7.5
. On the other hand, Lemma
7.6
(taking the
\limsup
as
\ell
tends to infinity)
ensures that

\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{k}(B_{F}(({\underline{\omega}},x),\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))\leqslant e^{-\mathbf{n}(h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma)}
.
Then, using the generalized entropy distribution principle we conclude that
h_{top}(F,I_{F}(\varphi))\geqslant h_{top}(F,\mathfrak{F})\geqslant h_{*}(\varphi)-5\gamma
.
As
\gamma>0
was chosen arbitrary, this shows that
h_{top}(F,I_{F}(\varphi))\geqslant h_{*}(\varphi)
, and completes the proof of Theorem
D
.

8.
Topological entropy of irregular points for the semigroup action

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
E
.
Let
X
be a compact metric space, assume that the semigroup action generated by the collection
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}
of bi-Lipschitz homeomorphisms has frequent hitting times, and let
\psi:X\to\mathbb{R}

be a continuous observable which is not a coboundary with respect to
G_{1}
.

8.1.
Ghys-Langevin-Walczak entropy of the set of irregular points

Here we prove item (1) in Theorem
E
.
It is immediate that

s((G,G_{1}),n,\varepsilon)\geqslant s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,\delta)

for every
n\geqslant 1
,
\varepsilon>0
,
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
and
0<\delta<1
. Our mild assumptions seem not to allow to construct
a single sequence
\tilde{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
whose number of irregular points for the observable
\psi
and the
sequential dynamical system
\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{\underline{\omega}}}

grows at least at the exponential rate of
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma
. Alternatively, we will produce sets
E_{n}\subset X
so that any
two points are
(n,\varepsilon)
-separated by some element
\underline{g}\in G_{n}
and whose Birkhoff averages of
\psi
determined by such
finite paths in the semigroup
G
do not converge. Let us formalize this strategy.

Assume that
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)>0
(otherwise there is nothing to prove)
and note that
H^{Katok}(\psi)=H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)>0

(recall (
4.12
)).
Hence, for any fixed
0<\gamma,\delta<1

there exist
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(F)
so that
\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}

and
h_{\mu_{i}}^{\delta}(F)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma
, for
i=1,2
.
Using (
4.10
), one knows that

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}},n,\varepsilon,\delta)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma

(8.1)

for
\mu_{i}
-almost every
{\underline{\omega}}
,
i=1,2
.
One can choose for each
i=1,2
a sequence
{\underline{\omega}}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
in the ergodic basin of attraction
of the
\sigma
-invariant probability measure
\pi_{*}\mu_{i}
satisfying (
8.1
).
The latter, together with the uniform continuity of
\psi
, allow us to select
\varepsilon>0
so that

|\psi(x)-\psi(x^{\prime})|<\frac{\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}}{4}

for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
so that
d(x,x^{\prime})<4\varepsilon
, and

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}}_{i},n,4\varepsilon,\delta)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma

for every
i=1,2
. Write
{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}={\underline{\omega}}_{1}
when

\ell\geqslant 1
is odd and
{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}={\underline{\omega}}_{2}
for an even
\ell\geqslant 1
.

Lemma 8.1
.

There exists a sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
of positive integers
such that:

(1)

n_{\ell}\geqslant\max\{\;\mathfrak{n}_{\ell},\;(\ell+2)\cdot n_{\ell-1}+\Big[\frac{2\log 2}{\log L}\Big],\;\ell\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell-1}K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\,\exp(K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\log\kappa)\;\}

for every
\ell\geqslant 2

(2)

for each
\ell\geqslant 1
there exists a
(g_{{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}},n_{\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\subset X

with cardinality

\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\geqslant\exp(\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma)n_{\ell}\,).

Proof.

The proof is identical to the one of Lemma
7.1
, the differences being that the sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell}
is required to grow exponentially
with respect to the terms
K(L^{-2n_{i-1}}\,\,2\varepsilon)
, and the separated sets are subsets of
X

instead of subsets of some fibers
X_{\underline{\omega}}
(which have a natural identification).
Moreover, since we need not consider iterations by the skew-product, we need not take the extra

\lfloor\log(\varepsilon^{-1})\rfloor
iterates necessary to control the dynamical distance with respect to the shift.
We shall omit the details.
∎

Now, note that the frequent hitting times property for the semigroup action ensures that
for each pair
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}
there exists

0\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})
and a finite word

\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}=\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}(x_{1},x_{2})\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{1}}
(depending on
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
)
so that

f^{p_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}(B(f^{n_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x),L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon))\cap B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(x_{2},n_{2},\varepsilon)

is a subset of
X
which contains a ball
B_{1}^{2}
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon
.
Thus

\displaystyle C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\,\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}(x_{1},x_{2}),\,{\underline{\omega}}_{2})}(\,x_{1},x_{2}\,)

\displaystyle:=B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;(\,f^{p_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\,)^{-1}\big(B_{1}^{2}\big)

(8.2)

is a compact and non-empty subset of
X
.
As the number of finite words of length at most
K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})
is bounded above by
K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})\,\kappa^{K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})}
, by the pigeonhole principle, for each
x_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
,
there exists

0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant K(L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon)
,
a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{t_{1}}
(depending only on
x_{1}
), and
a collection
\mathcal{\tilde{C}}_{2}
of elements of the form (
8.2
)
with
\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}(x_{1},x_{2})={\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}
and

\#{\mathcal{C}_{2}}\geqslant\#\mathcal{S}_{1}\cdot\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{2}}{K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})\,\exp(K(L^{-2{n_{1}}})\log\kappa)}.

In other words, the family
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is so that map making the transition between the finite piece of orbit of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
is
the same
f^{t_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}
, where
{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}
depends exclusively on
x_{1}
.
Let

\mathcal{I}_{2}=\big\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\colon C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\,{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},\,{\underline{\omega}}_{2})}(\,x_{1},x_{2}\,)\in\mathcal{C}_{2}\big\}.

Proceeding inductively we obtain, for each
k\geqslant 2
, a collection
\mathcal{I}_{k}\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}

so that

(1)

for each
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
and

1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there are

0\leqslant t_{i}\leqslant K(L^{-2n_{i}}\varepsilon)
and a finite word
{\underline{\omega}}^{(i(i+1))}\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{t_{i}}
(depending only on
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{i-1}
),
and a ball
B_{i}^{i+1}\subset B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i+1}}(x_{i+1},n_{i+1},\varepsilon)
of radius
\frac{1}{2}L^{-n_{i+1}}\varepsilon
so that

\displaystyle C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)

\displaystyle\qquad:=B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;\bigcap_{i=1}^{k-1}(\,f^{p_{i}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(i(i+1)}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}^{n_{i}}\circ\dots\circ f^{p_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\,)^{-1}(B_{i}^{i+1})

(8.3)

is a non-empty subset of
X
;

(2)

the collection
\mathcal{C}_{k}
of elements of the form (
8.3
), parameterized by
\mathcal{I}_{k}
, satisfies

\#{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\geqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{2}\,\#\mathcal{S}_{1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\,\exp(K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\log\kappa)}.

For each
k\geqslant 1
let

m_{k}:=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+t_{i})

and notice that although the transition length
t_{i}

(1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1)
may vary with the elements in
\mathcal{I}_{k}
, by construction this
is constant for any to
k
-uples
(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
so that
x_{j}=y_{j}
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant i-1
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Given
k\geqslant 1
,
if
E_{k}\subset X
is a collection of points, each one selected among a single element of
\mathcal{C}_{k}
then

E_{k}
is
(m_{k},\varepsilon)
-separated by elements of
G
.

Proof.

Fix
k\geqslant 1
and
let
E_{k}\subset X
be an arbitrary collection of points, each one selected among a single element of
\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
Recall that, for each
\ell\geqslant 1
, the set
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\subset X
is
(g_{{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}},n_{\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated. In consequence, the dynamic balls

\{B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}(x,n_{i},\varepsilon)\colon x\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\}
are pairwise disjoint, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
,
and the collection
{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
is formed by a disjoint union of compact sets of the form (
8.3
).
Let
x,y
belong to distinct elements in
{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
and let
(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
be so that

x\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\;\text{and}\;y\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,).

The previous construction ensures that if
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
is the smallest integer so that
x_{i}\neq y_{i}
then
{\underline{\omega}}^{j(j+1)}=\hat{\underline{\omega}}^{j(j+1)}

for every
1\leqslant j\leqslant j-1
. In particular,
g=f^{n_{i}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}\circ\dots\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}^{n_{2}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)}}^{t_{1}}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\in G_{m_{i}}

satisfies
d(g(x),g(y))>\varepsilon
. As
G_{m_{i}}\subset G_{m_{k}}
we conclude that

E_{k}
is
(m_{k},\varepsilon)
-separated by elements of
G
, as claimed.
∎

As
(\mathcal{C}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is a nested collection of disjoint and compact sets, the Moran set

\mathfrak{F}=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\;\Big[\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k}}\,C\Big]

is a non-empty compact subset of
X
.
Furthermore, as
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{m_{k}}{n_{k}}=1
(recall item (1) in Lemma
8.1
),
using the information of Lemma
8.2
in (
4.4
) one concludes that

\displaystyle h_{\operatorname{top}}^{GLW}(\mathbb{S},\mathfrak{F})

\displaystyle\geqslant\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\log s(G,G_{1},\mathfrak{F},m_{k},\varepsilon)\geqslant\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\log\#{\mathcal{C}_{k}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\log\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{2}\,\#\mathcal{S}_{1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\,\exp(K(L^{-2{n_{i}}}2\varepsilon)\log\kappa)}

\displaystyle\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma.

Thus, in order to complete the proof of item (1) in Theorem
E
it remains to prove the following:

Lemma 8.3
.

\mathfrak{F}
is contained in the irregular set
I_{\mathbb{S}}(\psi)
, defined by (
2.1
).

Proof.

For every
x\in\mathfrak{F}
there exists a nested sequence
(C_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}

of compact sets

C_{k}=C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}^{(12)},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},{\underline{\omega}}^{(23)},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}

so that
x\in\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}C_{k}
.
The choice of constants in Lemma
8.1
guarantees, along an argument identical to the one in the proof of
Lemma
6.7
,
that if
{\underline{\omega}}\in\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}[{\underline{\omega}}_{1}\mid_{[0,n_{1}]}{\underline{\omega}}^{12}{\underline{\omega}}_{2}\mid_{[0,n_{2}]}{\underline{\omega}}^{23}{\underline{\omega}}_{3}\mid_{[0,n_{3}]}\dots{\underline{\omega}}_{k}\mid_{[0,n_{k}]}]

then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(f^{j}_{{\underline{\omega}}}(x))

does not exist and, consequently,
x\in I_{\mathbb{S}}(\psi)
.
∎

8.2.
Bufetov entropy of the set of irregular points

Here we prove item (2) in Theorem
E
, namely that
h^{B}(\mathbb{S},I_{\mathbb{S}}(\psi))\geqslant h_{*}(\varphi_{\psi})
.
Indeed, using that
X
is compact and
[
40
, Theorem 5.1]
(see also Remark 2.4 in
[
83
]
),
if
\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}=\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{N}}
then

\overline{Ch}_{\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}\times Z}(F)=\log\kappa+\overline{Ch_{\text{top}}}(\mathbb{S},Z,\mathbb{P}_{sym})

for every
Z\subset X
(the term in the left-hand side is the topological capacity of the skew-product from the aforementioned references
and the right-hand side innvolves the topological capacity of the semigroup as in Subsection
4.2
).
In particular

\overline{Ch}_{\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}\times I_{\mathbb{S}}(\psi)}(F)=\log\kappa+\overline{Ch}_{I_{\mathbb{S}}(\varphi)}(\mathbb{S}).

By compactness of
X
,

h_{\text{top}}(F)=\overline{Ch_{\operatorname{top}}}(F,\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}\times X)=\log\kappa+\overline{Ch_{\text{top}}}(\mathbb{S},X,\mathbb{P}_{sym})=\log\kappa+h^{B}(\mathbb{S},\mathbb{P}_{sym})

Combining these two expressions with Theorem
D
we conclude that

h_{\operatorname{top}}(F)\geqslant\overline{Ch}_{X}(F)=\overline{Ch}_{I_{\mathbb{S}}(\varphi)}(F)\geqslant h_{*}(\varphi),

as desired. Since the two-sided shift
\Sigma_{\kappa}
is the natural extension of
\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}
, hence preserves the same topological entropy,
the previous results remain unaltered when
\Sigma_{\kappa}^{+}
is replaced by
\Sigma_{\kappa}
.
This finishes the proof of Theorem
E
.

9.
Topological entropy of fibered irregular sets

This subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
F
.
Let
X
be a compact metric space, assume that the semigroup action
\mathbb{S}
is generated by the collection
G_{1}=\{id,f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{\kappa}\}

of bi-Lipschitz homeomorphisms and let
\psi:X\to\mathbb{R}

be a continuous observable so that
\varphi_{\psi}
is not a coboundary with respect to
F
.
Assume also that there exists a strongly transitive homeomorphism
f_{i}\in G_{1}
, for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant\kappa
.
We will assume without loss of generality that
i=\kappa
.
In particular, by compactness of
X
, for each
\delta>0
there exists

N_{\delta}\geqslant 1
so that

\bigcup_{j=0}^{N_{\delta}}f_{\kappa}^{j}(B(x,\delta))=X\qquad\text{for every $x\in X$}.

(9.1)

Let
{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}(\delta)>0
denote the Lebesgue covering number of
\{f_{\kappa}^{j}(B(x,\delta))\}_{j=0}^{N_{\delta}}
. By compactness of
x
,
there exists
{\mathfrak{l}}_{\delta}:=\min_{x\in X}{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}(\delta)>0
so that:

\forall x,y\in X,\,\forall 0<\varepsilon<{\mathfrak{l}}_{\delta},\exists\,0\leqslant p\leqslant N_{\delta}

s.t.
f_{\kappa}^{p}(B(x,\delta))\supset B(y,\varepsilon)
.

(9.2)

Remark 9.1
.

The latter implies that the semigroup
\mathbb{S}
has the frequent hitting times
property where the transition maps can be obtained by a certain number of iterates of the map
f_{\kappa}
.
The expression (
9.1
) can be compared to the quantitative estimates obtained in Remark
6.4

in the special case that
f_{\kappa}
is an isometry.

9.1.
Fibered complexity of the set of irregular points

Here we prove item (1), namely that
h^{path}_{I}(\mathbb{S},\psi)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)
.
Assume that
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)>0
(otherwise there is nothing to prove).
By equality (
4.12
) we know that
H^{Katok}(\psi)=H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)>0
.
Hence, for any fixed
0<\gamma,\delta<1

there exist
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(F)
so that
\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}

and
h_{\mu_{i}}^{\delta}(F)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma
, for
i=1,2
.
Using the equality (
4.11
) and the ergodic theorem,
one concludes that there are
{\underline{\omega}}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
so that:

(a)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\delta_{\sigma^{j}({\underline{\omega}}_{i})}=\pi_{*}\mu_{i}

(b)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}^{j}(x))=\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{i}
for
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}
-a.e.
x\in X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}

(c)

h_{\mu}^{\delta}({\underline{\omega}}_{i})>H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma

for
i=1,2
, where for some abuse of notation we denote by
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}
denotes the sample measure of
\mu_{i}
on
X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}

obtained by Rokhlin’s disintegration theorem.

Since
\gamma>0
was chosen arbitrary, in order to prove the claim
it is sufficient to construct

{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
and a fibered Moran subset of the fiber
X_{\underline{\omega}}
with entropy larger than
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-\gamma
.
Such a construction differs from the ones at Sections
7
and
8
in the sense that,
in order to capture information created by the relative entropies

h_{\mu_{i}}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)
, the point
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
is constructed inductively. In fact, we will provide a sequence of integers
(m_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
,
a nested sequence of cylinders
[\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\dots,\omega_{m_{k}}]
,
and for each
\tilde{\underline{\omega}}\in\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}[\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\dots,\omega_{m_{k}}]
a Moran set

\mathfrak{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}\subset X_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}

so that

h_{\mathfrak{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}})\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-\gamma

(here
h_{\mathfrak{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}}(\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}})
denotes the fibered relative entropy of the set
\mathfrak{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}
, recall Subsection
4.3
). Albeit the construction of the Cantor sets
\mathfrak{F}_{\widetilde{{\underline{\omega}}}}
has a different flavor from the construction of the Cantor
set in the previous section, we will use some of the technical tools developed in the latter sections. We will emphasize the differences in comparison to the
previous construction and skim the details on the transition times.

9.1.1.
Fibered orbit growth estimates

Consider a strictly decreasing sequence of positive numbers
(\delta_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
tending to zero so that

\delta_{1}<\frac{1}{4}[\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}].

Associated to these, item (b) above together with Lusin’s theorem allows one to pick a strictly increasing sequence of large positive integers

(\mathfrak{n}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
so that,
for each
\ell\geqslant 1
, the fibered sets

\Gamma_{2\ell-1}:=\Big\{x\in X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell-1},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell-1}\Big\}

and

\Gamma_{2\ell}:=\Big\{x\in X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi(g_{\underline{\omega}}^{j}(x))-\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell}\Big\}

satisfy
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(\Gamma_{2\ell-1})>\frac{1}{2}
and
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(\Gamma_{2\ell})>\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover, using item (c) above and the definition of the fibered Katok
\delta
-entropy (taking
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
in (
4.10
)) one can choose
\varepsilon>0
small so that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}}_{1},n,8\varepsilon,\Gamma_{2\ell-1})>H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma

(9.3)

and

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}}_{2},n,8\varepsilon,\Gamma_{2\ell})>H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\gamma.

(9.4)

For each
i\geqslant 3
we take
{\underline{\omega}}_{i}={\underline{\omega}}_{1}
if
i
is odd and set
{\underline{\omega}}_{i}={\underline{\omega}}_{2}
otherwise.
By uniform continuity of
\psi
, reducing
\varepsilon
is necessary, we may also assume that

\displaystyle|\psi(x)-\psi(x^{\prime})|<\frac{\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}

(9.5)

for every
x,x^{\prime}\in X
so that
d(x,x^{\prime})<4\varepsilon
.
The next lemma provides a lower bound estimate on the cardinality of separated sets within the family of sets
(\Gamma_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

There exists a sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
of positive integers
satisfying

n_{\ell}\geqslant\max\{\;\mathfrak{n}_{\ell},\;(\ell+2)\cdot n_{\ell-1}+\Big[\frac{2\log 2}{\log L}\Big],\;\ell\cdot(\log\varepsilon^{-1}-\log{\mathfrak{l}}_{L^{-2{n_{\ell-1}}}\varepsilon}),\;\ell\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot N_{L^{-2{n_{\ell-1}}}\varepsilon}\;\}

(9.6)

for every
\ell\geqslant 2
, and so that for each
\ell\geqslant 1
there exists an
({\underline{\omega}}_{\ell},n_{\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\subset\Gamma_{\ell}

with cardinality

\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\geqslant\exp(\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma)n_{\ell}\,).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
7.1
, and for that reason we shall omit it.
∎

9.1.2.
Construction of fibered Moran sets of irregular points

We proceed to construct a point
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
and a special Moran subset of
X_{\underline{\omega}}
formed by points with irregular behavior.
By Lemma
9.2
, given
k\geqslant 1
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
one can choose a
({\underline{\omega}}_{i},n_{i},4\varepsilon)
-separated set
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\Gamma_{i}
so that
\#\mathcal{S}_{i}\geqslant\exp(\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma)n_{i}\,).

Given
x_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
, one knows that

B(f^{n_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1}),L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon)\subset f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}(\,B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\,)\quad\text{and}\quad B(x_{2},L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon)\subset B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(x_{2},n_{2},\varepsilon).

The choice of constants in (
9.6
) guarantees that
L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon
is smaller than the Lebesgue covering number
{\mathfrak{l}}_{L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon}
.
Thus, according to
(
9.2
),
one can pick
0\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon}
so that

f_{\kappa}^{p_{1}}(B(f^{n_{1}}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1}),L^{-2{n_{1}}}\varepsilon))\supset B(x_{2},L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon).

Thus

\displaystyle C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\,\kappa^{p_{1}},\,{\underline{\omega}}_{2})}(\,x_{1},x_{2}\,)

\displaystyle:=B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;(\,f^{p_{1}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\,)^{-1}\big(B(x_{2},L^{-n_{2}}\varepsilon)\big)

is a compact and non-empty subset of
X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}

and

\displaystyle(f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}^{n_{2}}\circ f^{p_{1}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}})(\,C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\,\kappa^{p_{1}},\,{\underline{\omega}}_{2})}(\,x_{1},x_{2}\,)\,)\supset B(f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}^{n_{2}}(x),L^{-2n_{2}}\varepsilon)

where
\kappa^{p_{1}}
denotes the
finite word
(\kappa,\kappa,\dots,\kappa)\in\{1,2,\dots,\kappa\}^{p_{1}}
.
Proceeding recursively, for each
k\geqslant 1
,
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}
and

1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
we obtain an integer
0\leqslant p_{i}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
so that

\displaystyle C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{p_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{p_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)

\displaystyle\qquad:=B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;\bigcap_{i=1}^{k-1}(\,f^{p_{i}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}^{n_{i}}\circ\dots\circ f^{p_{1}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\,)^{-1}(\,B(x_{i+1},L^{-n_{i+1}}\varepsilon)\,)

is a non-empty subset of
X_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}
.
The next result ensures that one can select a large number of points for which the transition times function
p_{i}=p_{i}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{i}\,)
is constant,
and use this to construct a suitable
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
whose non-autonomous dynamical system
\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}}

has a irregular set with large entropy.

Lemma 9.3
.

There exists
K_{0}>0
and for each
k\geqslant 1
there exists
a collection
\mathcal{I}_{k}\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}
such that:

(1)

for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there exists
1\leqslant t_{i}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
so that

p_{i}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)=t_{k}
for every
(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,

(2)

\#{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\geqslant K_{0}\,e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,m_{k}}

(3)

d_{\underline{\omega}}^{m_{k}}(x,y)>2\varepsilon

for any points
x\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)
and

y\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)
associated to distinct

k
-uples
(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
,

where
m_{k}=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+t_{i})
and
{\underline{\omega}}\in\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}[{\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k}]
.

Proof.

By the pigeonhole principle, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there exists

0\leqslant t_{i}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
and a collection

\mathcal{I}_{k}\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}
such that
item (1) above holds and, recalling that
n_{i}\gg N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{m_{k}}{n_{k}}=1

(recall (
9.6
)),

\displaystyle\#{\mathcal{I}_{k}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{2}\,\#\mathcal{S}_{1}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\,N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\gamma)\,[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{n_{i+1}}\,\frac{n_{i+1}}{N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,m_{k}}

for every large
k\geqslant 1
, where
m_{k}=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+t_{i})
.
In particular,
\#{\mathcal{I}}_{k}\geqslant Ke^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,m_{k}}
for every
k\geqslant 1
.
Now, if
{\underline{\omega}}\in\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}[{\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k}]

and
x\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)
and

y\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)
are points associated to distinct

k
-uples
(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}

and
j=\inf\{1\leqslant i\leqslant k\colon x_{i}\neq y_{i}\}
then

d_{\underline{\omega}}^{m_{k}}(x,y)\geqslant d(x_{j},y_{j})-d(f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{m_{j}}(x),x_{j})-d(f_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{m_{j}}(y),y_{j})>\varepsilon.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The sequence
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{k}
above was determined by the concatenation of the cylinders

{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}_{[0,n_{i}]}

and the cylinders determined by the finite words
\kappa^{p_{i}}
, i.e.,

{\underline{\omega}}\in({{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}_{[0,n_{1}]}\cdot\kappa^{p_{1}})\,\cap\,\bigcap_{i\geqslant 1}\sigma^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}(p_{j}+n_{j})}\;({{\underline{\omega}}_{i+1}}_{[0,n_{i+1}]}\cdot\kappa^{p_{i+1}}).

(9.7)

Consider also the sets

\mathfrak{F}_{m_{k}}({\underline{\omega}})=\bigcup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}_{k}}C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{p_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{p_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)

identified as a subset of
X_{\underline{\omega}}
,
where
m_{k}=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+t_{i})
, and the Moran set

\mathfrak{F}_{{{\underline{\omega}}}}:=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{F}_{m_{k}}({\underline{\omega}})\;\cap\;X_{{{\underline{\omega}}}}.

Lemma 9.4
.

\mathfrak{F}_{{{\underline{\omega}}}}
is contained in the irregular set
I_{\underline{\omega}}(\psi)
defined by (
2.10
).

Proof.

The argument follows the lines of the proof of Lemma
6.7
, and for that reason we shall omit it.
∎

9.1.3.
The relative entropy of
\mathfrak{F}_{\underline{\omega}}

In order to provide lower bounds for the fiber topological entropy of the fibered Moran set
\mathfrak{F_{\underline{\omega}}}
for the non-autonomous dynamical system

\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}}
we will make use of the
fibered version of the entropy distribution principle (cf. Proposition
4.4
).
For each
k\geqslant 1
consider the probability measure
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}

supported on points selected among the elements of sets in the family

\mathcal{C}_{k}=\{C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{p_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{p_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\colon(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\}.

More precisely,
for each
C\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
choose a point
z_{C}\in C

and consider
the probability measure

\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k}=\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k}}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k}}\delta_{z_{C}}.

Lemma 9.5
.

Any weak
∗
accumulation point
\nu_{\underline{\omega}}
of the sequence
(\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k})_{k\geqslant 1}

satisfies
\nu_{\underline{\omega}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\underline{\omega}})=1
.

Proof.

Let
\nu_{\underline{\omega}}
be an weak
∗
accumulation point of the sequence
(\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k})_{k\geqslant 1}
.
Given
k\geq 1
, using Portmanteau theorem on the characterization of weak
∗
convergence, as
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},\ell}(\mathfrak{F}_{m_{k}}({\underline{\omega}}))=1
for every
\ell\geqslant k
then

\nu_{{\underline{\omega}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{m_{k}}({\underline{\omega}}))=1
. Thus
\nu_{{\underline{\omega}}}\big(\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{F}_{m_{k}}({\underline{\omega}})\big)=1

as claimed.
∎

Now, let
\varepsilon>0
be small and
\mathbf{n}\geqslant 1
be fixed and large. Let
B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
be a dynamic ball which intersects the set
\mathfrak{F}_{\underline{\omega}}
and let
\ell=\ell(\mathbf{n})\geqslant 1
be so that

m_{\ell}\leqslant\mathbf{n}<m_{\ell+1}
, where the sequence
(m_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
was defined in Lemma
9.3
.
In order to apply Proposition
4.4
we need the following:

Lemma 9.6
.

\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k+\ell}(B_{\underline{\omega}}((x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))\leqslant e^{-\mathbf{n}(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-5\gamma)}
for every large
k\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k+\ell}(B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))>0
(otherwise there is nothing to prove), hence

B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)
intersects
\mathfrak{F}_{{\underline{\omega}},m_{k+\ell}}
.
We observe that if
y_{1}\in B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\cap C_{1}
and
y_{2}\in B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\cap C_{2}
where
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}

then item (3) in Lemma
9.3
, guarantees that

d_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{\mathbf{n}}(y_{1},y_{2})\geqslant d_{{\underline{\omega}}}^{m_{\ell}}(y_{1},y_{2})>2\varepsilon
whenever
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are distinct. Thus,
all points in the set
B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\cap\mathfrak{F}_{{\underline{\omega}},m_{k+\ell}}

belong to a single fixed set
C_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
.
Furthermore,
the construction in the proof of Lemma
9.3
yields that, for every
D\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}
,

\#\Big\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap D\neq\emptyset\Big\}\geqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{S}_{k+\ell}\,\dots\,\#\mathcal{S}_{\ell+1}}{\prod_{i=\ell}^{k+\ell-1}\,N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}}>e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}

for every large
k
. In particular, using that

\displaystyle\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}{\min\limits_{D\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell}}\;\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap D\neq\emptyset\}}

\displaystyle<e^{-(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}\;\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell},

that

m_{k+\ell}>\mathbf{n}
, that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{m_{k}}{n_{k}}=1
and the choices at item (1) in Lemma
9.3
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mu_{{\underline{\omega}},k+\ell}(B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon))

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\end{subarray}}\delta_{z_{C}}\left(B_{\underline{\omega}}(x,\mathbf{n},\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\displaystyle\frac{\#\{C\in\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}\colon C\cap C_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}}{\#\mathcal{C}_{k+\ell}}

\displaystyle<e^{-(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k+\ell}+\dots+n_{k+1}]}

\displaystyle<e^{-(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-5\gamma)\,m_{k+\ell}}<e^{-\mathbf{n}\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-5\gamma)}

for every large
k\geqslant 1
, thus concluding the proof of the lemma.

∎

We can now complete the proof of item (1) in Theorem
F
. Indeed, as the assumptions of the entropy distribution principle are satisfied
(as a consequence of Lemmas
9.5
and
9.6
above)
we conclude that

h^{path}_{I}(\mathbb{S},\psi)\geqslant h_{I_{\underline{\omega}}(\psi)}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-5\gamma.

As
\gamma>0
was chosen arbitrary, we get that
h^{path}_{I}(\mathbb{S},\psi)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)
, as desired.

9.2.
Moran sets of irregular points with large fibered entropy

In this subsection we explain the necessary modifications in the argument explored in the previous subsections of in order to prove item (2) in
Theorem
F
, namely that the topological entropy of the set
\Sigma:=\{{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon h_{I_{\underline{\omega}}(\psi)}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)\}

is larger or equal than
H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)
.

Fix
\gamma>0
. By definition (cf. (
2.9
)) there exists
\zeta>0
so that
H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi,\zeta)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-\gamma
. In particular there exist
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(F)
so that
\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}
,

\displaystyle h_{\mu_{i}}(F\mid\sigma)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-\zeta\quad\text{and}\quad h_{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}(\sigma)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-\gamma

(9.8)

for
i=1,2
. As the probability measures
\mu_{i}
are ergodic (taking
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
in (
4.11
)) it follows that

h_{\mu_{i}}^{1/4}(\underline{\omega})=h_{\mu_{i}}(F\!\mid\!\sigma)\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-\zeta

for
(\pi)_{*}\mu_{i}
-almost every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
.
Fix
0<\delta<\frac{1}{8}
. By the definition of the fibered Katok
entropy (recall (
4.10
)) and
Lusin’s theorem, for each
i=1,2
there exists
\tilde{\Omega}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}
and
\varepsilon>0
small so that
(\pi_{*}\mu_{i})(\tilde{\Omega}_{i})>1-\delta
and

\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log s({\underline{\omega}},n,4\varepsilon,\frac{1}{4})>H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-2\zeta,

for every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\tilde{\Omega}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Using Lusin’s theorem once more, we may take an integer
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\geqslant 1
and a subset
\Omega_{i}\subset\tilde{\Omega}_{i}
so that
(\pi_{*}\mu_{i})(\Omega_{i})\geqslant(1-2\delta)>\frac{3}{4}
and

s({\underline{\omega}},n,4\varepsilon,\frac{1}{4})>e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\zeta)n}\quad\text{for every $n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{0}$, ${\underline{\omega}}\in\Omega_{i}$ and $i=1,2$.}

(9.9)

We diminish
\varepsilon
, if necessary, so that

|\psi(x)-\psi(x^{\prime})|<\frac{\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}}{4}

whenever
d(x,x^{\prime})<4\varepsilon
.

Now we turn our attention to the convergence of Birkhoff averages on
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.
Consider a strictly decreasing sequence of positive numbers
(\delta_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
tending to zero so that

\delta_{1}<\frac{1}{4}[\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{2}-\int\varphi_{\psi}\,d\mu_{1}].

Using Birkhoff ergodic theorem and Lusin theorem, there exists a strictly increasing sequence of large positive integers

(\mathfrak{n}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
so that
\mathfrak{n}_{1}>\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and,
for each
\ell\geqslant 1
, the sets

\Gamma_{2\ell-1}:=\Big\{({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{1}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell-1},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell-1}\Big\}

and

\Gamma_{2\ell}:=\Big\{({\underline{\omega}},x)\in\{1,2,...,\kappa\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times X:\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F^{j}({\underline{\omega}},x))-\int\varphi\,d\mu_{2}\Big|<\delta_{2\ell},\;\;\forall n\geqslant\mathfrak{n}_{2\ell}\Big\}

satisfy
\mu_{1}(\Gamma_{2\ell-1})>1-\delta
and
\mu_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2}}(\Gamma_{2\ell})>1-\delta
for every
\ell\geqslant 1
.
These sets are not suitable to our purposes as we need to decouple information on the shift space
\Sigma_{\kappa}
and
the fibers. This is done as follows.

Let
\mu_{i}=(\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}})_{\underline{\omega}}
be the disintegration of
\mu_{i}
in sample measures given by Rokhlin’s disintegration theorem. More precisely,
there exists a
\mu_{i}
-almost everywhere defined and measurable family of probability measures
{\underline{\omega}}\mapsto\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}

so that
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}
and

\mu_{i}(A)=\int_{\Sigma_{\kappa}}\,\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}(A\cap X_{\underline{\omega}})\,d(\pi_{*}\mu_{i})({\underline{\omega}})
for every measurable set
A\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
.
We will use the following quantitative estimate.

Lemma 9.7
.

For each
\ell\geqslant 1
there exist subsets
\Xi_{2\ell-1},\Xi_{2\ell}\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}
so that

(1)

(\pi_{*}\mu_{1})(\Xi_{2\ell-1})>\frac{1}{4}
and
(\pi_{*}\mu_{2})(\Xi_{2\ell})>\frac{1}{4}
,

(2)

\mu_{1,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\big)>\frac{1}{4}

for every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Xi_{2\ell-1}
, and

(3)

\mu_{2,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell}\big)>\frac{1}{4}

for every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Xi_{2\ell}
.

Proof.

We use the following Fubini type estimate
involving to the sample measures
(\mu_{1,{\underline{\omega}}})_{\underline{\omega}}
:

\displaystyle 1-\delta<\mu_{1}(\Gamma_{2\ell-1})=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\kappa}}\,\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1})\,d(\pi_{*}\mu_{1})({\underline{\omega}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\pi_{*}\mu_{1}\Big({\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\big)>a\Big)

\displaystyle+a\cdot\pi_{*}\mu_{1}\Big({\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\big)\leqslant a\Big)

and, consequently,

\pi_{*}\mu_{1}\Big({\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\big)>a\Big)>1-\delta-a

for every
0<a<1
.
The choice
0<\delta<\frac{1}{4}
guarantees that

\pi_{*}\mu_{1}\Big({\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\colon\mu_{i,{\underline{\omega}}}\big(X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\big)>\frac{1}{4}\Big)>\frac{1}{2}.

The claim concerning the sample measures
\mu_{2,{\underline{\omega}}}
follows similarly.
∎

Observe that
\pi_{*}\mu_{1}(\Omega_{1}\cap\Xi_{2\ell-1})>1-2\delta-\frac{1}{4}>0
and
\pi_{*}\mu_{2}(\Omega_{2}\cap\Xi_{2\ell})>1-2\delta-\frac{1}{4}>0

for every
\ell\geqslant 1
.
Moreover, by Katok’s entropy formula (recall (
4.1
))
one can compute
h_{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}(\sigma)
(
i=1,2
) using
the sets
\Omega_{1}\cap\Xi_{2\ell-1}
and
\Omega_{2}\cap\Xi_{2\ell}
:

h_{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}(\sigma)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\;\Big[\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log N^{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,1-2\delta-\frac{1}{4})\,\Big],

where
N^{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}_{n}(8\varepsilon,1-2\delta-\frac{1}{4})
stands for the minimal cardinality of an
(n,8\varepsilon)
-generating subset for a subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}

of
(\pi_{*}\mu_{i})
-measure at least
1-2\delta-\frac{1}{4}
.
In particular, for each
\ell\geqslant 1

and
\varepsilon>0
is small then there exist
(n,4\varepsilon)
-separated sets
\Xi_{2\ell-1,n}\subset\Omega_{1}\cap\Xi_{2\ell-1}
and
\Xi_{2\ell,n}\subset\Omega_{2}\cap\Xi_{2\ell}
such that

\#\Xi_{2\ell,n}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)n}\quad\text{and}\quad\#\Xi_{2\ell-1,n}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)n}

for every large
n\geqslant 1
(depending on
\ell
).
Moreover, combining the latter with (
9.9
),
and item (2) in Lemma
9.7

one can produce a strictly increasing sequence
(n_{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
of positive integers
satisfying (
9.6
) and such that for each
\ell\geqslant 1
:

(i)

\#\Xi_{2\ell,n_{2\ell}}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)n_{2\ell}}\quad\text{and}\quad\#\Xi_{2\ell-1,n_{2\ell-1}}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)n_{2\ell-1}}

(ii)

for each
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Xi_{2\ell-1}
there exists a
({\underline{\omega}},n_{2\ell-1},4\varepsilon)
-separated set

\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}},2\ell-1}\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell-1}\quad\text{so that}\quad\#\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}},2\ell-1}\geqslant\exp(\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\zeta)n_{2\ell-1}\,).

(iii)

for each
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Xi_{2\ell}
there exists a
({\underline{\omega}},n_{2\ell},4\varepsilon)
-separated set

\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}},2\ell}\subset X_{\underline{\omega}}\cap\Gamma_{2\ell}\quad\text{so that}\quad\#\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}},2\ell}\geqslant\exp(\,(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-3\zeta)n_{2\ell}\,).

These informations can now be used to construct a Moran subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
of skew-product type whose both the projection on
\Sigma_{\kappa}

and the fibers have large entropy (see Figure 8.1 below for an illustration).

Figure 9.1.
Construction of a specific Moran subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
, formed from typical points for
\mu_{1}
(represented in gray) and typical points for
\mu_{2}
(represented in brown).

.

More precisely:

Proposition 9.8
.

There exists
K>0
and a sequence
(\mathfrak{S}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
of compact subsets of
\Sigma_{\kappa}
and a sequence
(\mathfrak{F}_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
of compact subsets of
\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
so that, for each
k\geqslant 1
:

(1)

\mathfrak{S}_{k}
is a finite union of cylinders in
\Sigma_{\kappa}
with the same length,

(2)

\mathfrak{F}_{k}\subset\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{S}_{k})
and for each
{\underline{\omega}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
there exist
m_{k}=m_{k}({\underline{\omega}})\geqslant 1
so that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{m_{k}}{n_{k}}=1
and
there exist
e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}
points in
\mathfrak{F}_{k}\cap X_{\underline{\omega}}

that are
({\underline{\omega}},m_{k},\varepsilon)
-separated,

(3)

The compact set

\mathfrak{F}:=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{F}_{k}\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X

is such that

\mathfrak{F}\,\cap\,X_{\underline{\omega}}\subset I_{\underline{\omega}}(\psi)
for every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\mathfrak{S}:=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,

(4)

h_{I_{\underline{\omega}}(\psi)}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})\geqslant H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-5\gamma
for every
x\in\mathfrak{S}
.

Proof.

This construction combines information from
Subsection
9.1
and the sets described in items (i)-(iii) above.
For each
k\geqslant 1
, each
k
-uple
({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\in\Xi_{1,n_{1}}\times\Xi_{2,n_{2}}\times\dots\times\Xi_{k,n_{k}}

and
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1},1}\times\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2},2}\times\dots\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{k},k}

and each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there exists an integer
0\leqslant p_{i}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
(depending on
{\underline{\omega}}_{j}
and
x_{j}
, for
1\leqslant j\leqslant i-1
)
so that

\displaystyle C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{p_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{p_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)

\displaystyle\qquad:=B_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}(x_{1},n_{1},\varepsilon)\;\cap\;\bigcap_{i=1}^{k-1}(\,f^{p_{i}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{i}}^{n_{i}}\circ\dots\circ f^{p_{1}}_{\kappa}\circ f_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}^{n_{1}}\,)^{-1}(\,B(x_{i+1},L^{-n_{i+1}}\varepsilon)\,)

is a non-empty subset of
X
. Proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
9.3
, one can extract
a large subcollection of these sets for which the transition time functions depend only on
{\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k}
. Indeed,
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k-1
there exists

0\leqslant t_{i}=t_{i}(({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{i}))\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}

and a collection

\mathcal{I}_{k}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\subset\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{1},1}\times\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{2},2}\times\dots\mathcal{S}_{{\underline{\omega}}_{k},k}
such that

\#\mathcal{I}_{k}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\geqslant Ke^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,m_{k}}

and

p_{i}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{i},{\underline{\omega}}_{1},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{i}\,)=t_{i}\quad\text{for every}\;(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k}).

Taking
m_{k}=n_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n_{i}+t_{i})
(which is a function of
({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})
), if

(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})
are distinct
k
-uples and one considers points

x\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)
and

y\in C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\,)
then

d_{\underline{\omega}}^{m_{k}}(x,y)>2\varepsilon
for any

{\underline{\omega}}\in\mathfrak{S}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k}):=({{\underline{\omega}}_{1}}_{[0,n_{1}]}\cdot\kappa^{t_{1}})\,\cap\,\bigcap_{i\geqslant 1}\sigma^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}(t_{j}+n_{j})}\;({{\underline{\omega}}_{i+1}}_{[0,n_{i+1}]}\cdot\kappa^{t_{i+1}}).

Notice that the cylinder set
\mathfrak{S}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}
has size
m_{k}
, which is a function of the transition times

(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{k-1})
given by the pigeonhole principle applied to the fibered sets.
There are

\#\Xi_{1,n_{1}}\times\#\Xi_{2,n_{2}}\times\dots\times\#\Xi_{k,n_{k}}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}

such cylinders in
\Sigma_{\kappa}
and, as
0\leqslant t_{i}\leqslant N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}
, using the pigeonhole principle once more we get
a subcollection
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
of such cylinder sets formed by cylinders in
\Sigma_{\kappa}
of the same length so that

\displaystyle\#{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\geqslant\frac{e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-2\gamma)[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\,N_{L^{-2{n_{i}}}\varepsilon}}\geqslant e^{(H^{\text{Pinsker}}_{\sigma}(\psi)-4\gamma)\,[n_{k}+\dots+n_{2}+n_{1}]}.

This allow us to consider also the nested sequence of compact sets
\mathfrak{F}_{k}\subset\Sigma_{\kappa}\times X
defined by

\mathfrak{F}_{k}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{S}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\;\bigcup_{(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}\mathfrak{S}({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})\times C_{({\underline{\omega}}_{1},\kappa^{t_{1}},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\kappa^{t_{2}},...,{\underline{\omega}}_{k})}(\,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\,),

as represented by Figure 8.1.
We emphasize that the transition times
t_{i}
are functions of
({\underline{\omega}}_{1},{\underline{\omega}}_{2},\dots,{\underline{\omega}}_{i})
.
These sets comply with the requirements of items (1) and (2) in the proposition.

By construction

\mathfrak{F}:=\bigcap_{k\geqslant 1}\mathfrak{F}_{k}

is a Moran set. It is not hard to check that the same ideas explored in previous sections ensure that items (3) and (4) also hold, we shall omit the details.
∎

10.
Linear cocycles

The main goal of this section is to prove Theorems
A
and
B

on the irregular behavior and rigidity for linear cocycles. First we shall comment on the fibered complexity of
the projective cocycle.

10.1.
Projective cocycles and complexities

The projective cocycle
P_{A}:\Sigma_{\kappa}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\Sigma_{\kappa}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
was defined in (
1.4
), and is a skew-product
with compact fibers.
Consider the continuous potential
\varphi_{A}:\Sigma_{\kappa}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\varphi_{A}({\underline{\omega}},v)=\log\frac{\|A({\underline{\omega}})\cdot v\|}{\|v\|},\quad\text{for}\;({\underline{\omega}},v)\in\Sigma_{\kappa}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(10.1)

Given
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(\sigma)
, if the cocycle
A
is strongly irreducible
then Furstenberg formula ensures that

\lambda_{+}(A,\nu)=\int\varphi_{A}({\underline{\omega}},v)\,d\eta(v)d\nu({\underline{\omega}})

where
\eta
is any
\nu
-stationary measure (see
[
32
]
or
[
80
, Theorem 6.8]
). While the previous expression allows to compute
the top Lyapunov exponent of the linear cocycle
A
as an average of a continuous potential, it does not provide any immediate regularity of the
Lyapunov exponent as a function of
\nu
as this relies ultimately on the analysis of the space of
\nu
-stationary measures as
\nu
varies.

Remark 10.1
.

As a consequence of the Oseledets theorem, if
{\underline{\omega}}
belongs to a total probability subset of
\Sigma_{\kappa}
then
for
every
normalized vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\varphi(F_{A}^{j}({\underline{\omega}},v))=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{\|A(\sigma^{j}({\underline{\omega}}))\cdot A^{(j)}({\underline{\omega}})v\|}{\|A^{(j)}({\underline{\omega}})v\|}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\frac{\|A^{(n)}({\underline{\omega}})v\|}{\|v\|}

does exist and coincides with one of the Lyapunov exponents
(\lambda_{i}(\mu))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
.

We now observe that the non-wandering set of each projective map
P_{A_{i}}:\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant\kappa
)
is contained in the projectivization of invariant subspaces of
A_{i}
. In fact, as a consequence of the Jordan canonical form theorem, we have the following
possibilities for the non-wandering set of the restriction of each map
P_{A_{i}}
to a projectivization of an eigenspace
E
associated to
A_{i}
:
(i) if
A_{i}\mid_{E}
has no nilpotent term then
P_{A_{i}}\mid_{\mathbf{P}E}
is the identify or an isometry in case of a real or complex eigenvalue, respectively;
(ii) if
A_{i}\mid_{E}
has nilpotent terms then there exists
F\subsetneq E
so that
{\mathbf{P}F}
is an attractor for
P_{A_{i}}\mid_{E}
and

P_{A_{i}}\mid_{\mathbf{P}F}
is the identify or an isometry in case of a real or complex eigenvalue, respectively.
In either case, the refinement of open coverings of
\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
under the action of
P_{A_{i}}
has sublinear growth, and the same holds for any
composition of such maps.Therefore,
h_{\mu}(P_{A})=h_{\pi_{*}\mu}(\sigma)
for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{inv}(P_{A})
and every non-autonomous dynamical system generated by these have zero topological entropy, ie,
h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})=0
.

Recall that for each
P_{A}
-invariant probability
\mu
one has that
h_{\mu}(P_{A})\leqslant h_{\pi_{*}\mu}(\sigma)+\int h_{\operatorname{top}}(\mathcal{F}_{\underline{\omega}})d\pi_{*}\mu
(cf.
[
52
]
).
As
H^{\text{Pinsker}}(\varphi_{A})=0
then

\displaystyle h_{*}(\varphi_{A})

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{c\geqslant 0\colon\text{there exist}\,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(P_{A})\,\text{so that}\hfill

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\,h_{\pi_{*}\mu_{i}}(\sigma)\geqslant c\;\text{and}\,\int\varphi_{A}\,d\mu_{1}<\int\varphi_{A}\,d\mu_{2}\Big\}

is bounded above by
h_{\operatorname{top}}(\sigma)=\log\kappa
.
The next lemma relates the top Lyapunov exponents with integrals for the projective cocycle by means of lifted measures.

Lemma 10.2
.

For every
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(\sigma)
there exists
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(P_{A})
so that

\int\varphi_{A}({\underline{\omega}},v)\,d\mu({\underline{\omega}},v)\lambda_{+}(A,\nu)

and

\pi_{*}\mu=\nu.

Proof.

First we construct invariant measures for the projective cocycle which can express the top Lyapunov exponent in an integral form.
Given
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{erg}(\sigma)
, the Oseledets theorem ensures there exists
1\leqslant\tilde{\kappa}\leqslant\kappa
and that for
\nu
-almost
every
{\underline{\omega}}\in\Sigma_{\kappa}
there exists a splitting
\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}=E^{1}_{\underline{\omega}}\oplus E^{2}_{\underline{\omega}}\oplus\dots\oplus E^{\tilde{\kappa}}_{\underline{\omega}}
, varying measurably with
{\underline{\omega}}
, so that
\lambda_{i}(A,\mu)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|A^{n}({\underline{\omega}})v\|
for every
v\in E^{i}_{\underline{\omega}}\setminus\{0\}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant\tilde{\kappa}
. As
\lambda^{+}(A,\nu)=\lambda_{1}(A,\nu)
and
\omega\mapsto\mathbf{P}E^{1}_{\underline{\omega}}
is a measurable family of submanifolds of

\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
of constant dimension,
one may consider the measurable family of probabilities
(\mu_{\underline{\omega}})_{\underline{\omega}}
so that
\operatorname{supp}\mu_{\underline{\omega}}\subset\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
and given by

\mu_{\underline{\omega}}(E)=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Leb}\mid_{\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times\mathbf{P}E^{1}_{\underline{\omega}})}(B)&,\text{if}\;\dim E^{1}_{\underline{\omega}}>1\\
\delta_{({\underline{\omega}},\mathbf{P}E^{1}_{\underline{\omega}})}(B)&,\text{otherwise}\end{array}\end{cases}

for every measurable set
B\in\{{\underline{\omega}}\}\times\mathbf{P}\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is clear that the probability
\mu=\int\mu_{\underline{\omega}}\,d\nu({\underline{\omega}})
is
P_{A}
-invariant,
possibly non-ergodic. By some abuse of notation we still denote by
\mu
an ergodic component of the previous probability.
By construction, and Remark
10.1
, one can check that

\int\varphi_{A}({\underline{\omega}},v)\,d\mu({\underline{\omega}},v)\lambda_{+}(A,\nu)
and
\pi_{*}\mu=\nu
,
as claimed.
∎

This lemma ensures that every
\sigma
-invariant and ergodic probability measure can be lifted to a
P_{A}
-invariant probability so that its Lyapunov exponent
can be computed as the time average of the potential
\varphi_{A}
according to its lift.
For general linear cocycles the upper semicontinuity of the top Lyapunov exponent
